To facilitate the rapid response of water utilities and reduce water losses caused by undiscovered leakages, a timely,\\r\\nefficient, and accurate detection method is required. This paper describes a novel framework for multiple overlapping leak detection and\\r\\nisolation in the water distribution system. The proposed method has three stages: an estimation stage to extract leak-induced data from the\\r\\nhuge monitoring data, an identification stage to detect the presence of overlapping leaks from multiscale difference series, and a localization\\r\\nstage to pinpoint the leak hotspots. The proposed method detects seven leakages in real-life networks and localizes 16 overlapping leakages\\r\\nwith an economic score of â‚¬167,981 in L-town 2019. Comparisons show that the framework can accurately detect it in a fast response time\\r\\nand effectively localize the leaking pipeline with good applicability. DOI: 10.1061/(ASCE)WR.1943-5452.0001558. Â© 2022 American\\r\\nSociety of Civil Engineers.\\r\\nAuthor keywords: Data-driven approach; Hydraulic model; Empirical mode decomposition; Time series decomposition; Multiscale\\r\\ndifference operation.\\r\\nIntroduction\\r\\nLeak detection in water distribution systems (WDSs) is a great\\r\\nchallenge for worldwide water utilities. Serious leakage accidents\\r\\ncause not only water losses but also significantly negative effects,\\r\\nsuch as the abrupt release of pressure and the intrusion of contam-\\r\\ninants through broken pipes (Wu et al. 2018; Xu et al. 2018). The\\r\\nkey to burst detection is to reduce the awareness time, i.e., the\\r\\nperiod between the burst occurrence and the awareness of water\\r\\nutilities. In short, timely leak detection is of vital importance for\\r\\nwater utilities.\\r\\nTo detect leakages in WDSs in time, quantities of equipment\\r\\nhave been applied for leak detection in the water industry, which\\r\\nmainly includes manual listening stick, standard correlator, and\\r\\ncorrelating noise loggers (Mutikanga et al. 2013; Shimanskiy et al.\\r\\n2003). Although the mentioned equipment can localize leakage\\r\\nprecisely, it remains a great challenge when facing the cost of time,\\r\\nmoney, and human power, and consequently resulting in a heavy\\r\\nburden on water utilities (Li et al. 2015).\\r\\nCompared with the presented methods relying on hardware\\r\\nequipment, detection based on the monitoring data in district me-\\r\\ntering areas (DMAs) costs less and arouses wide concern in the\\r\\nwater industry for its convenience. The monitoring data reflect the\\r\\noperating states of the network. When leaks happen, the operating\\r\\npatterns of monitoring data will be different from the ones under\\r\\nnormal conditions. By mining the DMA monitoring data, leakage\\r\\ncan be identified in real-time (Romano et al. 2014). The data-driven\\r\\nmethod can be mainly divided into three types, including statistical\\r\\nprocess control (SPC), classification, and prediction-classification\\r\\ntwo-stage method (Wu and Liu 2017b). Jung et al. (2015) propose\\r\\nand demonstrate the application of several SPC methods on de-\\r\\ntecting leaks. Pressure and flow data are analyzed through standard\\r\\ndeviation, cumulative sum (CUSUM), and threshold settings to\\r\\ndetect the abnormal statistical status caused by leakages. These\\r\\nSPC-based methods have been considered as simple, concise, and\\r\\ndirect techniques for leak detection (Ahn and Jung 2019). Some\\r\\nresearchers treat leakage detection as a binary classification prob-\\r\\nlem. By training a classification model based on machine learning,\\r\\nthe monitoring data are divided into normal and leak (Machell et al.\\r\\n2006). Furthermore, since the raising of the artificial neural net-\\r\\nwork (ANN) prediction model for networks (Mounce et al. 2002),\\r\\nthe two-stage method based on the ANNs has been a research focus\\r\\nin the water supply industry. This method employs historical data\\r\\nsuch as pressure and flow to train the prediction model and then\\r\\nclassifies according to the deviation between predicted and moni-\\r\\ntored data (Bakker et al. 2014a, b, 2013; Hutton and Kapelan 2015;\\r\\nJung and Lansey 2015; Mounce et al. 2011; Wang et al. 2020; Wu\\r\\n2009; Ye and Fenner 2014). The mentioned methods are all driven\\r\\nby data to characterize leakage data and normal data and do not rely\\r\\non the accuracy of the hydraulic model. The data-driven methods\\r\\ncan achieve a highly precise detection of leakages and locate the\\r\\nleak hotspot in the relatively small computation areas. However, the\\r\\npurpose of data-driven methods is to localize the leak in a small\\r\\narea (such as a DMA) but cannot pinpoint the accurate location of\\r\\nthe leaking pipeline.\\r\\n1Ph.D. Student, Smart Water Research Center, School of Environment,\\r\\nTsinghua Univ., Beijing 100084, China. This paper is part of the Journal of Water Resources\\r\\nPlanning and Management, Â© ASCE, ISSN 0733-9496.\\r\\nÂ© ASCE\\r\\n04022021-1\\r\\nJ. Water Resour. Plann. Manage.\\r\\n J. Water Resour. Plann. Manage., 2022, 148(6): 04022021 \\r\\n Downloaded from ascelibrary.org by New Jersey Institute of Technology on 10/15/26. \\r\\nCopyright ASCE; all rights reserved, including rights for text and data mining and training of artificial intelligence or similar technologies. \\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n=== PDF PAGE 2 ===\\r\\nBesides the presented methods, leakage detection based on hy-\\r\\ndraulics models is a popular kind of technique. By solving the pres-\\r\\nsure and node demand, the hydraulic model can simulate the real\\r\\noperation situation of water supply networks. Some researchers\\r\\nstimulate leakages many times and obtain all node leaksâ€™ character-\\r\\nistic values as reference. Then, they compare the hydraulic values\\r\\nwith monitoring one and search for the most possible leakage hot-\\r\\nspot in the area (Farley et al. 2013; Kang et al. 2014; Xie et al.\\r\\n2019). Some researchers detect leakages during the calibration of\\r\\nthe hydraulic model. Through genetic algorithms (Sanz et al. 2016;\\r\\nSavic et al. 2009; Xu et al. 2013) or other optimization algorithms,\\r\\nthe difference between simulated value and the monitored value\\r\\nis minimized, and the emitter coefficient of each node is solved.\\r\\nThe size of the emitter coefficient reflects the possibility of leak-\\r\\nage at the corresponding node (Wu 2009; Wu et al. 2010). Other\\r\\nresearchers combine the hydraulic model with ANN. The ANN\\r\\nis trained with large amounts of simulated leaks data to build a re-\\r\\nfection between the monitoring data and the leak hotspots (Zhou\\r\\net al. 2019). Based on the presented discussion, the hydraulic model\\r\\nis effective for judging the hotspots of leakages and is a good tool\\r\\nfor leakage detection. However, in addition to being limited by\\r\\nthe accuracy of the hydraulic model, the effectiveness of the\\r\\nhydraulic model is also limited by an important factor, the uncer-\\r\\ntainties of the actual pipe system and hydraulic model system.\\r\\nFluctuations in the demand cause different stochastic deviations\\r\\non the pressures in the real-life system and simulated system\\r\\n(Steffelbauer et al. 2014), and the authors refer to these kinds of\\r\\nstochastic deviations as uncertainties. Uncertainties often constitute\\r\\nthe noise of the objective function and further affect the accuracy of\\r\\nlocalization.\\r\\nIt is noteworthy that most real scenarios of pipe leakage are\\r\\na mixture of incipient leakage and abrupt leakage. However,\\r\\nthe mixture scenarios have not received much attention in leak\\r\\ndetection given its concealment and complexity for overlapping\\r\\nleakages. Given that the urgent need for the detection of real\\r\\nscenarios, it is imperative to detect and isolate multiple leak-\\r\\nages by proposing an efficient, accurate, and timely approach\\r\\nin WDSs.\\r\\nTo fill these research gaps, this paper aims to develop a novel\\r\\nestimation, identification, and localization framework (EILF) based\\r\\non the data-driven method and hydraulic model to detect multi-\\r\\nple overlapping leaks inside the DMA. The EILF comprises three\\r\\nstages. In the estimation stage, a joint estimation method and the\\r\\ntime series decomposition are proposed to highlight the flow and\\r\\npressure features related to leakage. In the identification stage,\\r\\na novel multiscale differential detection is proposed to reduce the\\r\\nnonstationary random item of residuals and to detect overlapping\\r\\nleakages. In the localization stage, a weekly difference-based\\r\\nsearch method is proposed to improve the ability to deal with\\r\\nuncertainty and to localize the leak hotspots. The framework is ap-\\r\\nplied to a real-life network and the L-town network to demonstrate\\r\\nits performance and practicality.\\r\\nMethodology\\r\\nFig. 1 shows the flow chart for EILF. The first step of EILF is\\r\\nto analyze the features of monitoring data and select appropriate\\r\\nestimation models that reduce the interference of normal water fluc-\\r\\ntuations on leak detection. The estimation of flow and pressure\\r\\nare described in detail in the â€œEstimation of Flow and Pressureâ€\\r\\nsection. The second step of EILF is to identify life cycle informa-\\r\\ntion of leakages, including leak start time, leak end time, leak type,\\r\\nand leak size, and the identification methods are described in\\r\\nthe â€œIdentification of Leakagesâ€ section. The final step of EILF\\r\\nis to locate the leaks and pinpoint the leak hotspots in the pipeline.\\r\\nDifferent from the previous pressure-based localization, an im-\\r\\nproved localized method based on weekly difference is introduced\\r\\nin the â€œLocalization of Leak Hotspotsâ€ section.\\r\\nEstimation of Flow and Pressure\\r\\nFlow and pressure are two vital hydraulic indicators, which are\\r\\nmonitored to provide effective guidance for analyzing the state\\r\\nof the pipe network. The variations in the flow or pressure data\\r\\nare caused by daily, weekly, and seasonal water usage patterns,\\r\\nleakages, and other uncertain factors. Previous studies have shown\\r\\nFig. 1. Flow chart of the estimation, identification, and localization framework (EILF).\\r\\nÂ© ASCE\\r\\n04022021-2\\r\\nJ. Water Resour. Plann. Manage.\\r\\n J. Water Resour. Plann. To facilitate the identification of leak-induced\\r\\ndata from the time series monitoring data, the anomalies caused by\\r\\nthe leakage need to be highlighted.\\r\\nDMA inflow has received wide attention in leak detection given\\r\\nits sensitivity for abnormal leak states. Leakage is the extra con-\\r\\nsumption apart from normal user consumption of DMA, whose es-\\r\\ntimation is undertaken by calculating the deviation between DMA\\r\\ninflow and user consumption. Therefore, the estimation of DMA\\r\\ninflow, or referred to as DMA water consumption under normal\\r\\nconditions, is the first procedure for leakage detection and isolation.\\r\\nThis section proposes a joint estimation method based on two\\r\\nDMAs in the same city, one nonleakage DMA-a is used to extract\\r\\nthe seasonal trend of this city, and one unidentified DMA-b is used\\r\\nto extract the weekly water consumption in a nonleaking state.\\r\\nBased on seasonal and weekly changes, the normal water consump-\\r\\ntion of DMA-b can be estimated.\\r\\nFor DMA-a, water consumption is a typical nonstationary time\\r\\nseries. Previous studies have shown that empirical mode decompo-\\r\\nsition (EMD) is an efficient tool for analyzing nonlinear and non-\\r\\nstationary data (Huang et al. 1998; Karthikeyan and Nagesh Kumar\\r\\n2013). In this study, EMD is proposed to extract the seasonal trend\\r\\nof normal water consumption. The authors assume that the water\\r\\nconsumption data are composed of trend items (changes within the\\r\\nseason), periodic items (changes within the week), and random\\r\\nitems (by stochastic factors), given as\\r\\nYÃ°tÃž Â¼ SÃ°tÃž Ã— TÃ°tÃž Ã— RÃ°tÃž\\r\\nÃ°1Ãž\\r\\nwhere YÃ°tÃž = normal water consumption of DMA-a; SÃ°tÃž = trend\\r\\nterm; TÃ°tÃž = periodic term; and RÃ°tÃž = random term in the data.\\r\\nIt should be noted that Eq. The residual term of the raw signal after EMD\\r\\nrepresents the seasonal trend of water consumption.\\r\\nFor DMA-b, the nonleakage week pattern can be extracted by\\r\\nthe demand pattern of the hydraulic model or the average of multi-\\r\\nple nonleakage weekly demands. Different\\r\\nfrom the flowmeters located at the entrance and exit of DMA, there\\r\\nare generally more correlated pressure sensors in the same DMA\\r\\narea (Wu and Liu 2017a; Xu et al. 2020). When a leak occurs in the\\r\\npipe network, the pressure at the node adjacent to the leak will de-\\r\\ncrease, and the closer the node is to the leak, the greater the pressure\\r\\ndrop. Similarly, when the leak is repaired, the closer the node is\\r\\nto the leak, the greater the pressure rise. Therefore, the pressure\\r\\nincrease or decrease, that is, the pressure trend at each node, is used\\r\\nas the highlight indicator of the leakage in this section.\\r\\nThe seasonal-trend decomposition procedure based on Loess\\r\\n(STL) is proposed to estimate the pressure trend from ordinary\\r\\npressure time series (Cleveland et al. 1990). The pressure time\\r\\nseries P is decomposed into three series including daily compo-\\r\\nnent D, trend component T, and residual component R, given as\\r\\nPiÃ°tÃž Â¼ DiÃ°tÃž Ã¾ TiÃ°tÃž Ã¾ RiÃ°tÃž\\r\\nÃ°3Ãž\\r\\nwhere TiÃ°tÃž = pressure trend time series at pressure sensor i in\\r\\nthe DMA-b.\\r\\nIdentification of Leakages\\r\\nLeakage is the extra consumption apart from the normal water\\r\\nconsumption in DMA. When a leak occurs, the SCADA system\\r\\nwill record online monitoring data containing the leak. The flow\\r\\nand pressure caused by the leakage are highlighted in the previous\\r\\nâ€œEstimation of Flow and Pressureâ€ section as (1) the flow residual\\r\\nbetween normal water consumption and the actual water consump-\\r\\ntion monitored by SCADA sensors; and (2) the pressure trend at\\r\\ndifferent nodes. In this section, the authors use the presented two\\r\\nfeatures to focus on solving two identification problems: (1) the\\r\\ndetection of leaks; and (2) the assessment of leaks.\\r\\nThe purpose of leak detection is to identify the start time, end\\r\\ntime, and volume of leaks and to provide zonal (or DMA-level) leak\\r\\ntips for the water utility. Previous studies have suggested that leak-\\r\\nage is more sensitive to flow than to pressure (Mounce et al. 2011;\\r\\nYe and Fenner 2011). Considering the timeliness and accuracy of\\r\\nleak identification, the flow residual series between normal water\\r\\nconsumption and the actual water consumption monitored by\\r\\nSCADA sensors is used for solving the first leak detection problem.\\r\\nFor flow residual data, the characteristics of the flow residual\\r\\nsequence should be analyzed first. Although the residuals series is\\r\\ncalculated by the difference between observed and estimated series,\\r\\nit is still a time series with irregular fluctuations. To reduce the non-\\r\\nstationary residual series and amplify the leakage signal implicit\\r\\nin the residual series, a multiscale difference method is proposed\\r\\nin this study. Difference operation, also known as finite difference,\\r\\ndifference function, or difference operation, is a concept in math-\\r\\nematics. It maps the original function fÃ°xÃž to fÃ°x Ã¾ aÃž âˆ’fÃ°x Ã¾ bÃž.\\r\\nDifference operation, corresponding to differential operation, is an\\r\\nimportant concept in calculus, which is commonly used for the\\r\\nsmoothing of nonstationary time series (Flajolet and Sedgewick\\r\\n1995). Corresponding to residual\\r\\ndata, the pattern of residual series contains constant (no leak),\\r\\nabrupt increase (abrupt leakage), incipient increase (incipient\\r\\nleakage), and abrupt decrease (repaired leakage, the end time of\\r\\nleakage). The abrupt increase and decrease are easily identified\\r\\nthrough difference transformation, and the three-sigma SPC limit\\r\\nis used to identify the sudden rise and incline of the flow curve\\r\\n(Jung et al. 2015; Wang et al. 2020). Compared with\\r\\nthe flow sensors, the pressure sensors are more widely distributed in\\r\\nthe network due to the low cost of installation. Therefore, pressure\\r\\ndata collected from sensors are more convenient and abundant\\r\\nfor leak localization. Combined with the network simulated by the\\r\\nhydraulic model, a common solution is to treat localizing as an\\r\\noptimization problem. The discrepancy of real-life and simulated\\r\\npressure is minimized as the objective function, and the leak hot-\\r\\nspots with minimized function can be localized through grid\\r\\nsearching. This pressure-based search method has been applied in\\r\\nmany previous studies; however, this kind of optimization inevi-\\r\\ntably has two different system uncertainties of real-life and simu-\\r\\nlated systems. Fluctuations in the demand cause different stochastic\\r\\ndeviations on the pressures in the real-life system and simulated\\r\\nsystem (Steffelbauer et al. 2014), and the authors refer to these\\r\\nkinds of stochastic deviations as uncertainties. Uncertainties often\\r\\nconstitute the noise of the objective function and further affect the\\r\\naccuracy of localization.\\r\\nFor solving the uncertainty optimization problem and con-\\r\\nsidering the weekly variation of water consumption, the weekly\\r\\ndifference method, which is commonly used to describe the varia-\\r\\ntion value of discrete variables, is used to describe the leakage\\r\\ncharacteristics. Difference operation is often used in the smooth\\r\\nprocessing of nonstationary time series. Its core is to eliminate\\r\\nthe trend and fluctuation of the system through subtraction so that\\r\\nthe time series becomes stable. The pressure data is a typical non-\\r\\nstationary time series with obvious weekly trends. To eliminate\\r\\nthe influence of weekly trends and uncertainties, the real-life and\\r\\nsimulated pressures are calculated by weekly difference operation\\r\\nseparately. The pressure value change after the weekly difference is\\r\\nselected as the leakage characteristic value. For each leakage, two\\r\\nweeks when the leakage had occurred (week occ) and when the\\r\\nleakage had not occurred (week nocc) are found. It is crucial to emphasize that assuming inputs from pressure sensors and calculating losses based on all nodes is impractical in real situations. That is, nodes to be generalized (i.e. non-sensor nodes) cannot be considered known during training to prevent information leakage [19,33]. Thus, despite the aim of this study is to achieve prediction of all nodes, the input dimension of ℎ𝑣𝐼,𝑡𝐽\n in Eq. (3) is 𝐼×𝐽\n, instead of (𝐼+𝐼*)×𝐽\n.\nThe training challenge lies in learning the transferable correlation between sensor measurements and nodal features. After acquiring the trained model of the 𝐼\n sensor nodes, forecasting all 𝐼+𝐼*\n nodes are accomplished by additionally inputting the 𝐹⁡ℎ𝑣𝐼*\n and 𝐹⁡𝑡𝑣𝐼*\n of the remaining 𝐼*\n non-sensor nodes:\n(4)\n𝐺⁡𝑒⁢𝑛⁢𝑒⁢𝑟⁢𝑎⁢𝑙⁢𝑖⁢𝑧⁢𝑖⁢𝑛⁢𝑔⁡(𝑝𝑣𝐼,𝑡𝐽+𝐹⁡ℎ𝑣𝐼+𝐼*+𝐹⁡𝑡𝑣𝐼+𝐼*)=𝑓Θ⁡(𝑝𝑣𝐼,𝑡𝐽+𝐹⁡ℎ𝑣𝐼+𝐼*+𝐹⁡𝑡𝑣𝐼+𝐼*)=ℎ𝑣𝐼+𝐼*,𝑡𝐽*\n2.2. Explainable forecasting framework\nThe GRU [6] is a highly optimized variant of the recurrent neural network. Its core advantage lies in its unique architectural design. Through the precise regulatory mechanisms of the update gate and reset gate, the GRU skillfully balances the retention and forgetting of historical information [7]. This allows it to capture the dynamics of long-term and short-term dependencies in time series data [24]. The GGNN, an innovative extension of the GRU [17], integrates recurrent layers, graph convolution, and gating mechanisms. It retains the advantages of the GRU in time series prediction while deeply analyzing the complex structure of non-Euclidean space graph data through graph convolution operations, achieving efficient utilization of graph data [31]. However, the GGNN also faces limitations of the convolution-based graph methods: Each iteration only focuses on the first-order neighborhood of the sensor nodes. Thus, the iteration of the recurrent layers restricts the generalization of sensor node features to a larger network space [32].\nBuilding upon the dynamic global weighting capability of attention mechanisms, this study enhances the GGNN framework by integrating self-attention and external-attention modules. The self-attention module addresses the limited ability of standard graph convolutions to capture long-range spatial dependencies among nodes, significantly improving spatial representational capacity. The external-attention module explicitly models the relationships between nodal attributes and pressure observations, enhancing the model's physical relevance to WDSs. Fig. 1 illustrates the overall AGN architecture. The subsequent sections detail the design and implementation of this framework:\nDownload: Download high-res image (318KB)\nDownload: Download full-size image\n\nFig. 1. The workflow of the proposed AGN model.\n\n(1) Neighbor propagation based on adjacency matrix: By multiplying candidate hidden pressure states (CHPS) 𝑐(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗\n with the weighted adjacency matrix 𝒲𝑣𝑖\n, the past CHPS of sensor node 𝑣𝑖\n is transferred to its first-order neighborhood nodes:\n((5))\n̅\n𝑐\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗=𝒲𝑣𝑖·𝑐(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗\nwhere 𝑙\n is the iteration of recurrent layers, and the initial CHPS 𝑐(0)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗\n is an initialized learnable parameter.\n(2) Long-short-term memory augmented by self-attention mechanism: By calculating the matrix product of the query vector 𝑄·⁡(·)\n and the key vector 𝐾·⁡(·)\n of the node sequence (Eqs. (6)-a and 7-a), the attention between node sequences can be reflected. The attention scores are obtained after normalization through SoftMax activation (Eqs. (6)-b and 7-b). The value vectors 𝑉·⁡(·)\n of all nodes in the sequence are weighted and summed using the attention scores. After passing through the sigmoid activation 𝜎⁡(·)\n, the retention weights of short-term memory 𝑟(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗\n and long-term memory 𝑢(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗\n can be obtained ((6), (7)):\n(6)\n𝑟(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗=𝜎⁢(\n̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅\n𝐴⁢𝑟\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗·𝑉𝑟⁡(𝑝(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗∥\n̅\n𝑐\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗))\n(6-a)\n𝐴⁢𝑟(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗=\n𝑄𝑟⁡(𝑝(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗∥\n̅\n𝑐\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗)·𝐾𝑟⁡(𝑝(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗∥\n̅\n𝑐\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗)\n√𝐷\n(6-b)\n̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅\n𝐴⁢𝑟\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗=\nexp⁡(𝐴⁢𝑟(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗)\n∑𝑣𝑖∈𝒱𝑒⁢𝑥⁢𝑝⁡(𝐴⁢𝑟(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗)\n(7)\n𝑢(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗=𝜎⁢(\n̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅\n𝐴⁢𝑢\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗·𝑉𝑢⁡(𝑝(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗∥\n̅\n𝑐\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗))\n(7-a)\n𝐴⁢𝑢(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗=\n𝑄𝑢⁡(𝑝(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗∥\n̅\n𝑐\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗),𝐾𝑢⁡(𝑝(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗∥\n̅\n𝑐\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗)\n√𝐷\n(7-b)\n̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅\n𝐴⁢𝑢\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗=\nexp⁡(𝐴⁢𝑢(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗)\n∑𝑣𝑖∈𝒱𝑒⁢𝑥⁢𝑝⁡(𝐴⁢𝑢(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗)\nwhere \n̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅\n𝐴⁢𝑟\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗\n and \n̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅\n𝐴⁢𝑢\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗\n are the normalized attention scores of the reset gate and update gate, respectively. 𝑄·⁡(·)\n, 𝐾·⁡(·)\n, and 𝑉·⁡(·)\n are three different linear transformations. The symbol ∥\n denotes the concatenation operation of pressure measurement 𝑝(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗\n and past CHPS \n̅\n𝑐\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗\n. 𝐷\n is the dimension of the concatenation, preventing gradient explosion.\nWhen the reset gate weight 𝑟(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗\napproaches 0, the model “forgets” more past information (i.e. CHPS). When it approaches 1, it “remembers” more. After obtaining 𝑟(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗\n, the past CHPS is reset (𝑟(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗⊙\n̅\n𝑐\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗)\n, and then the current CHPS \ñ\n𝑐\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗\n can be obtained:\n(8)\ñ\n𝑐\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗=𝑡⁢𝑎⁢𝑛⁢ℎ⁡(\n̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅\n𝐴⁢𝑐\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗·𝑉𝑐⁡(𝑝(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗∥(𝑟(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗⊙\n̅\n𝑐\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗)))\n(8-a)\n𝐴⁢𝑐(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗=\n𝑄𝑐⁡(𝑝(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗∥(𝑟(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗⊙\n̅\n𝑐\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗)),𝐾𝑐⁡(𝑝(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗∥(𝑟(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗⊙\n̅\n𝑐\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗))\n√𝐷\n(8-b)\n̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅\n𝐴⁢𝑐\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗=\nexp⁡(𝐴⁢𝑐(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗)\n∑𝑣𝑖∈𝒱𝑒⁢𝑥⁢𝑝⁡(𝐴⁢𝑐(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗)\nThe update gate weight 𝑢(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗\n represents the mixing ratio of past and current information. If the update gate value approaches 0, the CHPS 𝑐(𝑙)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗\n will be mainly determined by the past CHPS \n̅\n𝑐\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗\n. Conversely, if the update gate weight approaches 1, the CHPS will be mainly determined by the current CHPS \ñ\n𝑐\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗\n, and the model will focus more on the current input 𝑝(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗\n:\n(9)\n𝑐(𝑙)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗=(1−𝑢(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗)⊙\n̅\n𝑐\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗+𝑢(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗⊙\ñ\n𝑐\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗\n(3) Hidden pressure feature transfer based on recurrence: By looping the steps described in Eq. (6) to Eq. (9), the learned CHPSs of sensor nodes are progressively generalized to non-sensor nodes through the weighted adjacency matrix.\n(4) Integration of nodal type and height features: By calculating the external-attention of the CHPSs to the nodal height and nodal type features, the potential nodal features can be obtained. These features are then fused with the CHPSs using addition and multiplication, respectively, to forecast the future nodal heads:\n(10)\nℎ(𝑙)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗=(𝑐(𝑙)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗+\ñ\n𝐹⁡ℎ\n𝑣𝑖)⊙\ñ\n𝐹⁡𝑡\n𝑣𝑖\nwhere potential height feature \ñ\n𝐹⁡ℎ\n𝑣𝑖\n and the potential type feature \ñ\n𝐹⁡𝑡\n𝑣𝑖\n are obtained by calculating the external-attention of CHPS 𝑐(𝑙)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗\n to nodal height 𝐹⁡ℎ𝑣𝑖\n (Eq. (11)) and nodal type 𝐹⁡𝑡𝑣𝑖\n (Eq. (12)) features, respectively:\n(11)\ñ\n𝐹⁡ℎ\n𝑣𝑖=\n̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅\n𝐴⁢𝐹⁡ℎ\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗·𝑉𝐹⁡ℎ⁡(𝐹⁡ℎ𝑣𝑖)\n(11-a)\n𝐴⁢𝐹⁡ℎ𝑣𝑖=\n𝑄𝐹⁡ℎ⁡(𝑐(𝑙)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗)·𝐾𝐹⁡ℎ⁡(𝐹⁡ℎ𝑣𝑖)\n√𝐷\n(11-b)\n̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅\n𝐴⁢𝐹⁡ℎ\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗=\nexp⁡(𝐴⁢𝐹⁡ℎ𝑣𝑖)\n∑𝑣𝑖∈𝒱𝑒⁢𝑥⁢𝑝⁡(𝐴⁢𝐹⁡ℎ𝑣𝑖)\n(12)\ñ\n𝐹⁡𝑡\n𝑣𝑖=\n̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅\n𝐴⁢𝐹⁡𝑡\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗·𝑉𝐹⁡𝑡⁡(𝐹⁡𝑡𝑣𝑖)\n(12-a)\n𝐴⁢𝐹⁡𝑡𝑣𝑖=\n𝑄𝐹⁡𝑡⁡(𝑐(𝑙)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗)·𝐾𝐹⁡𝑡⁡(𝐹⁡𝑡𝑣𝑖)\n√𝐷\n(12-b)\n̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅\n𝐴⁢𝐹⁡𝑡\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗=\nexp⁡(𝐴⁢𝐹⁡𝑡𝑣𝑖)\n∑𝑣𝑖∈𝒱𝑒⁢𝑥⁢𝑝⁡(𝐴⁢𝐹⁡𝑡𝑣𝑖)\n2.3. Cascade masking operation\nPrior research [18] demonstrates that masking operations improve model generalization in water network applications. The core strategy involves randomly selecting a proportion of sensor nodes’ input during training. These inputs are then set to zero, simulating prediction scenarios for non-monitored sites. This approach effectively enhances the model’s ability to generalize to indirectly observed nodes, making it an effective method for data augmentation.\nBuilding on this concept, this study introduces the cascade masking operation as a novel enhancement strategy. Renowned for its multi-level refinement in complex image processing tasks [2,3], such as instance segmentation, cascade masking can progressively enhance both segmentation accuracy and robustness. This study adapts this advanced concept to graph processing by incrementally decreasing the number of randomly masked sensor measurements and their features during training. This staged approach systematically strengthens the model's ability to capture complex interdependencies between sensor data and network topology, significantly improving generalization to unmonitored nodes.\n3. Experimental setups\n3.1. Dataset\nReal-world WDSs typically lack comprehensive nodal monitoring, preventing direct validation of demand forecasts at non-instrumented nodes. To address this limitation and assess model performance under realistic conditions, this study adopt the benchmark l-Town Network dataset [30]. As depicted in Fig. 2(a), this network has substantial elevation variations, with a maximum difference of 74.2 m, and is serviced by two water tanks. It includes notable high and low areas where water pressure is regulated by a pressurized pumping station and a pressure-reducing valve, respectively. This network essentially covers the characteristics found in typical real WDSs.\nDownload: Download high-res image (1MB)\nDownload: Download full-size image\n\nFig. 2. Nodal features of l-Town Network. (a) Nodal height features (b) Nodal type features (c) Variation of different water usage types over a year.\n\nThe l-Town benchmark network comprises 50 predefined residential and commercial water demand patterns, along with 4 industrial demand patterns. To enhance the model’s relevance to real-world scenarios, this study slightly modified the l-Town network. Specifically, this study randomly assigned these 104 demand patterns to the 782 demand nodes. Fig. 2(b) depicts the water usage type at each demand node; Fig. 2(c) illustrates all water demand pattern curves, with grey lines representing individual patterns and the red line indicating their mean values. A total of one year of sequence data with a time step of 5 min was generated. The training, validation, and testing datasets were constructed by chronologically assigning 70 %, 20 %, and 10 % of the total samples, respectively.\nThe pressurized pumping station that provides additional pressure to the high zone is represented as a node with known pressures for prediction. Its nodal pressures were obtained from pump characteristic curves and flow sensors installed on the pump. In the weighted adjacency matrix, the two pipelines connected to the “pumping station node” are represented by a sufficiently large number (300 in this example), while the pressure-reducing valve is represented by a sufficiently small number (1 in this example).\n3.2. Model settings\nFor the forecasting tasks, this study adopted the GHR proposed by Zhou et al. [37] and the GGNN used by Li et al. [18] to effectively compare our proposed AGN model with previous studies. Especially, this study combined the GHR’s state estimation results with the GRU time prediction algorithm (GHR+GRU) to achieve forecasting task and extended the GGNN from water quality to the task of nodal demand.\nFor all three models (AGN, GGNN, and GRU), the hidden layer dimension was set to 12. All three neural networks were trained using the ADAM optimizer with a training batch size of 32. The initial learning rate was set to 1e-3, and it was reduced by a factor of 0.8 after 1 training epoch. If the training error does not decrease over 10 batches, the epoch will be terminated early. For the AGN and GGNN, the number of recurrent layers was set to 3. The forecasting step length is set to be equal to the entered historical step length.\n3.3. Evaluation metric\nThis study reported the Mean Absolute Error (MAE) and Mean Absolute Percentage Error (MAPE) of forecasting results for all 783 nodes (including 1 “pumping station node”) in different forecasting time steps, to evaluate the model's spatial generalization and time series forecasting effect. The formula is as follows:\n(13)\n𝑀⁢𝐴⁢𝐸=\n1\n(𝐼+𝐼*)×𝐽*\n⁢\n𝐼+𝐼*\n∑\n𝑖=1\n𝐽*\n∑\n𝑗=1\n∣ℎ𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗−\ñ\nℎ\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗∣\n(14)\n𝑀⁢𝐴⁢𝑃⁢𝐸=\n1\n(𝐼+𝐼*)×𝐽*\n⁢\n𝐼+𝐼*\n∑\n𝑖=1\n𝐽*\n∑\n𝑗=1\n∣ℎ𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗−\ñ\nℎ\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗∣\nℎ𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗\nwhere ℎ𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗\n is the prediction and \ñ\nℎ\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗\n is the simulated measurement of vector 𝑣𝑖\n at time step 𝑡𝑗\n.\n4. Results and discussion\nThis section presents the results from both forecasting performance and model explainability analyses. First, this study report and analyze the forecasting task results: the proposed method achieves superior spatial generalization and temporal forecasting accuracy compared to previous approaches. Subsequently, this study evaluates the robustness of the AGN model across varying prediction windows. Finally, once demonstrated the quality of our predictions, this study explains the recurrent layers, attention mechanisms and nodal features used in the model, demonstrating how these components influence the final output of the model.\n4.1. Forecasting results\n4.1.1. Spatial generalization accuracy\nThis study aims to utilize the GNN algorithm to predict the water demand states from a pressure perspective. A thorough review of previous research revealed that, although several pioneering studies have explored the estimation or forecasting of WDS states without relying on pre-built hydraulic models, they still face limitations. Specifically, the GHR method proposed by Zhou et al. [37] is restricted to instantaneous state estimation, and its accuracy heavily depends on sensor placement; the direct application of the GGNN algorithm does not integrate well with the characteristics of water networks [18]. To address these shortcomings, this study introduces the AGN method. This innovative approach incorporates the attention mechanisms into hidden state representation and node feature extraction. By doing so, it overcomes the limitations of traditional recurrent graph convolution methods and aligns closely with the unique pressure characteristics of water networks. To evaluate the performance of the AGN, the GGNN method and a GRU-enhanced GHR approach were employed as benchmark models for comparison.\nFig. 3 visually demonstrates AGN's superior performance on the test set against its benchmark counterparts: the GGNN method and the GRU-enhanced GHR (i.e. GHR+GRU) approach. Specifically, the MAE of node predictions was categorized into three regions, clearly revealing the significant advantage of the AGN model in spatial generalization accuracy. Notably, 82.8 % of total nodes have an MAE below 0.5 m, a widely recognized threshold for practical application in hydraulic model calibration. Compared to competing models, AGN shows a significant advantage in the proportion of nodes below this threshold, outperforming GGNN and GHR+GRU methods by 79 % and 73.4 %, respectively, highlighting its superior spatial generalization performance.\nFurther analysis of Fig. 3(b) reveals a profound correlation between prediction error and nodal head variation. Nodes with errors between 0.5 m and 1.0 m are mainly concentrated in high-elevation areas, while low-elevation nodes generally face challenges with MAE greater than 2.0 m. This phenomenon indicates an imbalance in model training, particularly as low-elevation nodes are not sufficiently learned due to sparse sensor placement. We attribute this primarily to two factors: (1) sensor scarcity in low-elevation regions hinders the model's ability to capture localized hydraulic characteristics, and (2) insufficient model capacity to discern elevation-driven hydraulic variations. These findings underscore the critical impact of sensor deployment density on data-driven model performance, highlighting the need for comprehensive data acquisition to optimize model training.\nDownload: Download high-res image (1MB)\nDownload: Download full-size image\n\nFig. 3. Comparison of forecasting models in spatial direction: (a) The real nodal heads variation; (b) The MAE loss of AGN model; (c) The MAE loss of GGNN model; (d) The MAE loss of GHR+GRU method.\n\nAdmittedly, in the face of complex water network components, drastic elevation changes, and sparse sensor deployment, the AGN model still faces considerable challenges in pursuing comprehensive and accurate predictions. However, it is noteworthy that even under such extreme conditions, the AGN model demonstrates remarkable predictive ability in the high-elevation area, despite the sensors in this area still being relatively sparse — 100 % of high-elevation nodes have an MAE below 1.0 m. In the context of low-density sensor deployment, the AGN can still effectively address sparse sensor environments, reducing reliance on sensing equipment. More real-world WDSs will benefit from our sensor-robust model.\nAs shown in Fig. 3(b), the AGN model achieves satisfactory prediction accuracy and breadth in the medium-elevation area. This suggests that in water distribution systems (WDSs) characterized by relatively gentle terrain and moderate head changes, the AGN model can deliver enhanced predictive performance. These results not only further consolidate the practicality and reliability of the AGN model but also lays a solid theoretical and practical foundation for its widespread application in complex WDS management, heralding a promising future for data-driven technology in intelligent WDS management.\nDue to their relatively low deployment and maintenance costs, pressure sensors have been widely used in WDSs. This provides a rich data source for the AGN model and practically verifies the applicability and feasibility of water demand prediction from pressure data. This practice-oriented research perspective offers new ideas and methodological support for WDS management.\n4.1.2. Temporal forecasting accuracy\nTo further analyze the fluctuations in prediction accuracy over time, Fig. 4 presents the MAPE variation for 328 non-overlapping test batches in a time series format. This visualization enhances our understanding of the model’s dynamic performance and highlights the superior long-term effectiveness of the AGN method. In each test batch, the MAPE of AGN remains impressively below 5 %, demonstrating significant advantages in both prediction accuracy and stability over the GGNN and GHR+GRU methods.\nDownload: Download high-res image (373KB)\nDownload: Download full-size image\n\nFig. 4. Comparison of forecasting models in temporal direction.\n\nNotably, the AGN model maintains stable and low MAPE levels even after more than a month of continuous testing. This remarkable performance is a strong testament to its robustness in time-series prediction. This stability is also crucial for practical applications, allowing the model to operate effectively long-term and significantly reduce dependence on computational resources.\nFurther comparative analysis reveals that, unlike AGN and GGNN which also incorporate GRU components, the GHR+GRU method exhibits significant instability in its time-series performance, with markedly increased fluctuation amplitudes. This phenomenon may indicate the limitations of the GHR method in handling water networks with extreme head variations and suboptimal sensor placement. It emphasizes the spatial generalization challenges of accurately predicting non-sensor nodes’ state based on limited sensor measurements. And this is the key challenge in data-driven water network modeling.\nAlthough this study achieves high-precision predictions with MAPE not exceeding 5 % over time, it seems unacceptable when faced with a wide head variation range of 41.1 m to 113.6 m (Fig. 3(a)). This is mainly due to the approximately 4 % of low-elevation nodes, whose MAPE reaches as high as 80 %, significantly raising the overall average MAPE. The error levels for different elevation regions are analyzed in detail in the next section.\nDespite these limitations, the results of the demand prediction displayed along the time axis clearly show the long-term robustness of the AGN model in practical deployment environments. Notably, the model was trained on a consumer-grade GPU platform (NVIDIA GeForce RTX 4060 Ti), requiring approximately 3 h in total. Leveraging the computational efficiency inherent to deep learning methods, the model achieves an inference time of <1 s per run, sufficiently satisfies the requirement for real-time prediction. These findings not only enhance our confidence in the model’s performance but also provides strong support and insights for its future application in broader and more complex scenarios. Subsequent sections analyze prediction accuracy variations across time steps and regions.\n4.1.3. Accuracy analysis at varying time steps\nTo thoroughly investigate the prediction accuracy of the proposed AGN model across three different elevation regions with varying prediction steps, we tested four different prediction steps and presented the MAE and MAPE for the low, medium, and high regions separately (Table 1). As the prediction step length increased, the error metrics for all three regions exhibited stability, with minimal variation observed. The overall error was primarily contributed by the fewer low-elevation nodes. The medium- and high-elevation nodes achieved satisfactory prediction results.\n\nTable 1. Forecasting with different prediction window sizes.\n\nForecasting steps\tRegion\tMAE (m)\tMAPE ( %)\n12\tHigh\t0.859\t1.12\nMedium\t0.327\t0.439\nLow\t32.7\t79.7\n24\tHigh\t0.885\t1.21\nMedium\t0.329\t0.441\nLow\t32.7\t79.6\n48\tHigh\t0.725\t0.985\nMedium\t0.370\t0.496\nLow\t32.6\t79.3\n96\tHigh\t0.692\t0.938\nMedium\t0.396\t0.531\nLow\t32.5\t79.0\nDetailed analysis of prediction accuracy across different regions at varying time steps revealed a distinct trend: as the prediction step length increased, prediction error for high-elevation nodes decreased, while error for medium-elevation nodes increased. This indicates that for high-elevation nodes experiencing data scarcity, extending the time series length and incorporating more prior temporal information enhances forecast accuracy. Conversely, for nodes in data-rich areas, additional temporal information may introduce unnecessary noise or redundancy, reducing model accuracy. This finding suggests that, for nodes in data-scarce conditions, incorporating more prior information and adopting more suitable data fusion techniques will be key to enhancing the model’s performance.\n4.2. Results explanation\n4.2.1. Rule extraction from recurrent layer\nEmploying recurrent graph convolution (as shown in Eq. (5)), the AGN model gradually expands the receptive field of sensor nodes’ neighboring nodes. Simultaneously, pressure feature transfer from sensor nodes to global nodes is achieved by the self-attention mechanism, enabling long-range generalization.\nTo understand the rule of recurrent layers and their impact on the model’s generalization ability, this study varied the number of recurrent layers and tested the model’s generalization accuracy under different iterations. Fig. 5(a) shows the relative position relationship between sensor nodes and non-sensor nodes. The size and color depth of the non-sensor nodes indicate their distance from the nearest sensor nodes. Additionally, the data statistics in the upper right corner reveal the shortest path distribution between sensor nodes and non-sensor nodes. Notably, most non-sensor nodes are three steps away from sensor nodes, with the longest path being eleven steps. This information provides a crucial basis for subsequent analysis.\nDownload: Download high-res image (827KB)\nDownload: Download full-size image\n\nFig. 5. Analysis of the number of the recurrent layers: (a) shortest path from sensor nodes to non-sensor nodes; (b) MAE distribution corresponding to different numbers of the recurrent layers; (c) MAPE corresponding to different numbers of the recurrent layers.\n\nExperimental results (Figs. 5(b) and 5(c)) indicate that the best performance is not achieved at the maximum iteration of 11, but at 3 iterations of recurrent layers. This finding demonstrates that more iterations are not necessarily better; instead, they need to match the majority of the shortest path between sensor nodes and non-sensor nodes. When the number of iterations exceeds a certain threshold (such as 1 in this case), further increasing the number of recurrent layers has limited improvement on the model’s prediction performance. This is primarily due to the attention mechanism’s ability to capture long-distance correlations, allowing nodes to transcend physical distance limitations. Through the self-attention mechanism, the model can learn and transfer the pressure characteristics from sensor nodes to all nodes, rather than being limited to the sensor nodes’ neighborhood. Therefore, spatial generalizing process can be summarized as two significant mechanisms: neighbor topology feature transferring based on graph convolution, and global feature generalization based on the self-attention mechanism. Based on these two key mechanisms, the AGN model can generalize the learned graph structure-related features to all nodes in WDS, achieving state forecasting.\nIn the GGNN, ensuring sensor nodes can “perceive” the farthest non-sensor nodes theoretically requires up to 11 steps of recurrent iterations. However, this approach presents significant practical challenges, particularly due to the substantial increase in training time. For example, training with 11 iterations of recurrent layers on an A10 tensor core GPU in the small-scale network studied here takes about two days. This duration is unacceptable for practical applications in large-scale WDSs, where non-sensor nodes are further away from their nearest sensor node. In contrast, using 3 recurrent layers reduces the training time to about 3 h, demonstrating significant efficiency advantages.\nFurther analysis reveals that increasing the number of recurrent layers from 1 to 2 significantly reduces the MAPE. However, increasing beyond 3 layers leads to a rise in MAPE. This suggests that too many recurrent layers may introduce excessive non-sensor node features, causing data redundancy or pollution, and weakening the model’s prediction performance. Additionally, regardless of the number of recurrent layers, the cumulative maximum MAE remains stable at 96.03 %, revealing an optimization bottleneck in specific regions, such as low-elevation region in this study. These regions, due to insufficient sensor placement, cannot improve training results by increasing the number of recurrent layers.\nIn summary, incorporating the self-attention mechanism in the recurrent layers significantly enhances the model’s prediction accuracy. It also effectively reduces the iteration of recurrent layers, greatly decreasing training time of the model. This advantage is crucial for predictive modeling of actual WDSs, especially for medium- and large-scale systems, where its efficiency will provide significant application value.\n4.2.2. Hidden semantic analysis of gating mechanisms\nThrough an in-depth analysis of the weight matrices obtained from the trained reset gate (Eq. (6)) and the update gate (Eq. (7)), this study reveals the unique mechanism by which the model selectively retains short- and long-term information between nodes. Analysis of the model parameters reveals that the weights of the two critical gates consistently remain close to 0.5. The balance between the reset gate and update gate indicates that short- and long-term memory are equally important in the forecasting model. This finding inspires a new perspective on model simplification: replacing complex gating mechanisms with several constants. This operation will significantly reduce the model’s parameters, thereby enhancing its performance and facilitating its application in larger-scale WDSs.\nThe CHPS (Eq. (8)) demonstrates how the AGN model efficiently retains key information. By visually mapping the attention weight of the hidden state layer onto the network (Fig. 6), we can observe how sensor nodes influence non-sensor nodes and how the model selectively focuses on feature correlation. For visualization purposes, only edges with weights greater than 0.01 are displayed in the figure, and the weights of nodes 215 were divided by 10. This analysis deepens our understanding of the model’s internal hidden mechanisms and highlights the impact of sensor layout on prediction performance.\nDownload: Download high-res image (2MB)\nDownload: Download full-size image\n\nFig. 6. Attention weight of the CHPS.\n\nThe study found significant differences in the contributions of various sensor nodes during the prediction process, reflecting their interdependence and the strategic significance of sensor layout. Intuitively, sensor nodes in central areas (e.g., 458, 549, and 752) enjoy higher weights due to their critical positions, significantly enhancing the overall prediction performance. In contrast, sensors at the network periphery (e.g., nodes 114, 469, and 769) are less important. The concepts of geographical center and periphery do not exist in graph theory; only the concept of connectivity does. In other words, nodes that exhibit better connectivity or play a crucial role in the network’s connectivity were trained with higher weights. Placing sensor nodes at critical connectivity points appears to be key to improving the performance of graph-based data-driven models. The importance related to node connectivity underscores the need to optimize sensor layouts, especially in selecting critical connections. However, further cross-network studies are needed to definitively establish the correlation between sensor placement and connectivity.\nBy introducing the self-attention mechanism into the gating mechanism, this study broadens the receptive field of sensor nodes and effectively captures global information. Additionally, it deepens our understanding of the working mechanisms of complex network prediction models. These findings provide theoretical support for optimizing existing models and lay a solid foundation for developing new models for future urban infrastructure monitoring and management.\n4.2.3. Attribution analysis based on nodal features\nBy separately masking nodal height and nodal type features, this study analyzed their independent and synergistic effects on the model’s prediction. Fig. 7 shows the prediction effects after masking these two features in the AGN model. The fourth quadrant of the figure summarizes the MAE and MAPE statistics for each masking scenario, providing quantitative evidence of feature contributions. The AGN model in Fig. 7(a), with both nodal type and height masked, is a modification of the GGNN (Fig. 3(c)), replacing all fully connected layers with self-attention layers. Experimental results show that the attention mechanism significantly expands the model’s receptive field, enabling 50.1 % of the nodes to be trained — a 14.7 % improvement over the original GGNN. These findings highlight the attention mechanism’s advantage in capturing long-distance correlations between nodes.\nDownload: Download high-res image (1009KB)\nDownload: Download full-size image\n\nFig. 7. Nodal features analysis: (a) Mask nodal type and nodal elevation features; (b) Mask nodal type features; (c) Mask nodal elevation features.\n\nAdding any individual nodal feature to the “AGN(-type)(-height)” scenario can significantly enhance the model’s overall prediction accuracy. Specifically, adding only the nodal height feature (Fig. 7(b)) increases the proportion of nodes with MAE<1.0 m from 0 % to 95.6 %. This indicates that height, a key indicator of spatial location information, provides extensive and fundamental predictive support for the model. Adding nodal type features (Fig. 7(c)) increases the proportion of nodes with MAE<0.5 m from 0 % to 23.2 %. This significantly enhances the model’s ability to capture specific nodal characteristics and improves prediction accuracy, demonstrating the unique value of type features in refining predictions. Both nodal elevation and type features offer distinct and complementary contributions to model performance. The results highlight the critical role of prior network information in improving model performance.\nHowever, in water networks lacking detailed nodal information, obtaining accurate nodal height and type data can be challenging. Therefore, promoting and deepening the AGN model in practical applications will require diversified model assistance and data substitution strategies. For instance, nodal height can be approximated using surface elevation, and nodal type can be inferred by integrating urban AOI data with network topology. With the inclusion and integration of more network prior information, the AGN model can better align with water network characteristics, becoming powerful tools for the safety management and distribution optimization of WDSs.\n5. Explainable forecasting framework\nThe GRU [6] is a highly optimized variant of the recurrent neural network. Its core advantage lies in its unique architectural design. Through the precise regulatory mechanisms of the update gate and reset gate, the GRU skillfully balances the retention and forgetting of historical information [7]. This allows it to capture the dynamics of long-term and short-term dependencies in time series data [24]. The GGNN, an innovative extension of the GRU [17], integrates recurrent layers, graph convolution, and gating mechanisms. It retains the advantages of the GRU in time series prediction while deeply analyzing the complex structure of non-Euclidean space graph data through graph convolution operations, achieving efficient utilization of graph data [31]. However, the GGNN also faces limitations of the convolution-based graph methods: Each iteration only focuses on the first-order neighborhood of the sensor nodes. Thus, the iteration of the recurrent layers restricts the generalization of sensor node features to a larger network space [32].\nBuilding upon the dynamic global weighting capability of attention mechanisms, this study enhances the GGNN framework by integrating self-attention and external-attention modules. The self-attention module addresses the limited ability of standard graph convolutions to capture long-range spatial dependencies among nodes, significantly improving spatial representational capacity. The external-attention module explicitly models the relationships between nodal attributes and pressure observations, enhancing the model's physical relevance to WDSs. Fig. 1 illustrates the overall AGN architecture.
