After passing through the sigmoid activation 𝜎⁡(·)\n, the retention weights of short-term memory 𝑟(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗\n and long-term memory 𝑢(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗\n can be obtained ((6), (7)):\n(6)\n𝑟(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗=𝜎⁢(\n̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅\n𝐴⁢𝑟\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗·𝑉𝑟⁡(𝑝(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗∥\n̅\n𝑐\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗))\n(6-a)\n𝐴⁢𝑟(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗=\n𝑄𝑟⁡(𝑝(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗∥\n̅\n𝑐\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗)·𝐾𝑟⁡(𝑝(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗∥\n̅\n𝑐\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗)\n√𝐷\n(6-b)\n̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅\n𝐴⁢𝑟\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗=\nexp⁡(𝐴⁢𝑟(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗)\n∑𝑣𝑖∈𝒱𝑒⁢𝑥⁢𝑝⁡(𝐴⁢𝑟(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗)\n(7)\n𝑢(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗=𝜎⁢(\n̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅\n𝐴⁢𝑢\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗·𝑉𝑢⁡(𝑝(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗∥\n̅\n𝑐\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗))\n(7-a)\n𝐴⁢𝑢(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗=\n𝑄𝑢⁡(𝑝(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗∥\n̅\n𝑐\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗),𝐾𝑢⁡(𝑝(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗∥\n̅\n𝑐\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗)\n√𝐷\n(7-b)\n̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅\n𝐴⁢𝑢\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗=\nexp⁡(𝐴⁢𝑢(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗)\n∑𝑣𝑖∈𝒱𝑒⁢𝑥⁢𝑝⁡(𝐴⁢𝑢(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗)\nwhere \n̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅\n𝐴⁢𝑟\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗\n and \n̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅\n𝐴⁢𝑢\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗\n are the normalized attention scores of the reset gate and update gate, respectively. 𝑄·⁡(·)\n, 𝐾·⁡(·)\n, and 𝑉·⁡(·)\n are three different linear transformations. The symbol ∥\n denotes the concatenation operation of pressure measurement 𝑝(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗\n and past CHPS \n̅\n𝑐\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗\n. 𝐷\n is the dimension of the concatenation, preventing gradient explosion.\nWhen the reset gate weight 𝑟(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗\napproaches 0, the model “forgets” more past information (i.e. CHPS). When it approaches 1, it “remembers” more. After obtaining 𝑟(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗\n, the past CHPS is reset (𝑟(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗⊙\n̅\n𝑐\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗)\n, and then the current CHPS \ñ\n𝑐\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗\n can be obtained:\n(8)\ñ\n𝑐\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗=𝑡⁢𝑎⁢𝑛⁢ℎ⁡(\n̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅\n𝐴⁢𝑐\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗·𝑉𝑐⁡(𝑝(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗∥(𝑟(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗⊙\n̅\n𝑐\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗)))\n(8-a)\n𝐴⁢𝑐(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗=\n𝑄𝑐⁡(𝑝(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗∥(𝑟(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗⊙\n̅\n𝑐\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗)),𝐾𝑐⁡(𝑝(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗∥(𝑟(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗⊙\n̅\n𝑐\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗))\n√𝐷\n(8-b)\n̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅\n𝐴⁢𝑐\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗=\nexp⁡(𝐴⁢𝑐(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗)\n∑𝑣𝑖∈𝒱𝑒⁢𝑥⁢𝑝⁡(𝐴⁢𝑐(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗)\nThe update gate weight 𝑢(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗\n represents the mixing ratio of past and current information. If the update gate value approaches 0, the CHPS 𝑐(𝑙)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗\n will be mainly determined by the past CHPS \n̅\n𝑐\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗\n. Conversely, if the update gate weight approaches 1, the CHPS will be mainly determined by the current CHPS \ñ\n𝑐\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗\n, and the model will focus more on the current input 𝑝(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗\n:\n(9)\n𝑐(𝑙)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗=(1−𝑢(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗)⊙\n̅\n𝑐\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗+𝑢(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗⊙\ñ\n𝑐\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗\n(3) Hidden pressure feature transfer based on recurrence: By looping the steps described in Eq. (6) to Eq. (9), the learned CHPSs of sensor nodes are progressively generalized to non-sensor nodes through the weighted adjacency matrix.\n(4) Integration of nodal type and height features: By calculating the external-attention of the CHPSs to the nodal height and nodal type features, the potential nodal features can be obtained. These features are then fused with the CHPSs using addition and multiplication, respectively, to forecast the future nodal heads:\n(10)\nℎ(𝑙)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗=(𝑐(𝑙)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗+\ñ\n𝐹⁡ℎ\n𝑣𝑖)⊙\ñ\n𝐹⁡𝑡\n𝑣𝑖\nwhere potential height feature \ñ\n𝐹⁡ℎ\n𝑣𝑖\n and the potential type feature \ñ\n𝐹⁡𝑡\n𝑣𝑖\n are obtained by calculating the external-attention of CHPS 𝑐(𝑙)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗\n to nodal height 𝐹⁡ℎ𝑣𝑖\n (Eq. (11)) and nodal type 𝐹⁡𝑡𝑣𝑖\n (Eq. (12)) features, respectively:\n(11)\ñ\n𝐹⁡ℎ\n𝑣𝑖=\n̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅\n𝐴⁢𝐹⁡ℎ\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗·𝑉𝐹⁡ℎ⁡(𝐹⁡ℎ𝑣𝑖)\n(11-a)\n𝐴⁢𝐹⁡ℎ𝑣𝑖=\n𝑄𝐹⁡ℎ⁡(𝑐(𝑙)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗)·𝐾𝐹⁡ℎ⁡(𝐹⁡ℎ𝑣𝑖)\n√𝐷\n(11-b)\n̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅\n𝐴⁢𝐹⁡ℎ\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗=\nexp⁡(𝐴⁢𝐹⁡ℎ𝑣𝑖)\n∑𝑣𝑖∈𝒱𝑒⁢𝑥⁢𝑝⁡(𝐴⁢𝐹⁡ℎ𝑣𝑖)\n(12)\ñ\n𝐹⁡𝑡\n𝑣𝑖=\n̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅\n𝐴⁢𝐹⁡𝑡\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗·𝑉𝐹⁡𝑡⁡(𝐹⁡𝑡𝑣𝑖)\n(12-a)\n𝐴⁢𝐹⁡𝑡𝑣𝑖=\n𝑄𝐹⁡𝑡⁡(𝑐(𝑙)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗)·𝐾𝐹⁡𝑡⁡(𝐹⁡𝑡𝑣𝑖)\n√𝐷\n(12-b)\n̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅\n𝐴⁢𝐹⁡𝑡\n(𝑙−1)\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗=\nexp⁡(𝐴⁢𝐹⁡𝑡𝑣𝑖)\n∑𝑣𝑖∈𝒱𝑒⁢𝑥⁢𝑝⁡(𝐴⁢𝐹⁡𝑡𝑣𝑖)\n2.3. Cascade masking operation\nPrior research [18] demonstrates that masking operations improve model generalization in water network applications. The core strategy involves randomly selecting a proportion of sensor nodes’ input during training. These inputs are then set to zero, simulating prediction scenarios for non-monitored sites. This approach effectively enhances the model’s ability to generalize to indirectly observed nodes, making it an effective method for data augmentation.\nBuilding on this concept, this study introduces the cascade masking operation as a novel enhancement strategy. Renowned for its multi-level refinement in complex image processing tasks [2,3], such as instance segmentation, cascade masking can progressively enhance both segmentation accuracy and robustness. This study adapts this advanced concept to graph processing by incrementally decreasing the number of randomly masked sensor measurements and their features during training. This staged approach systematically strengthens the model's ability to capture complex interdependencies between sensor data and network topology, significantly improving generalization to unmonitored nodes.\n3. Experimental setups\n3.1. Dataset\nReal-world WDSs typically lack comprehensive nodal monitoring, preventing direct validation of demand forecasts at non-instrumented nodes. To address this limitation and assess model performance under realistic conditions, this study adopt the benchmark l-Town Network dataset [30]. As depicted in Fig. 2(a), this network has substantial elevation variations, with a maximum difference of 74.2 m, and is serviced by two water tanks. It includes notable high and low areas where water pressure is regulated by a pressurized pumping station and a pressure-reducing valve, respectively. This network essentially covers the characteristics found in typical real WDSs.\nDownload: Download high-res image (1MB)\nDownload: Download full-size image\n\nFig. 2. Nodal features of l-Town Network. (a) Nodal height features (b) Nodal type features (c) Variation of different water usage types over a year.\n\nThe l-Town benchmark network comprises 50 predefined residential and commercial water demand patterns, along with 4 industrial demand patterns. To enhance the model’s relevance to real-world scenarios, this study slightly modified the l-Town network. Specifically, this study randomly assigned these 104 demand patterns to the 782 demand nodes. Fig. 2(b) depicts the water usage type at each demand node; Fig. 2(c) illustrates all water demand pattern curves, with grey lines representing individual patterns and the red line indicating their mean values. A total of one year of sequence data with a time step of 5 min was generated. The training, validation, and testing datasets were constructed by chronologically assigning 70 %, 20 %, and 10 % of the total samples, respectively.\nThe pressurized pumping station that provides additional pressure to the high zone is represented as a node with known pressures for prediction. Its nodal pressures were obtained from pump characteristic curves and flow sensors installed on the pump. In the weighted adjacency matrix, the two pipelines connected to the “pumping station node” are represented by a sufficiently large number (300 in this example), while the pressure-reducing valve is represented by a sufficiently small number (1 in this example).\n3.2. Model settings\nFor the forecasting tasks, this study adopted the GHR proposed by Zhou et al. [37] and the GGNN used by Li et al. [18] to effectively compare our proposed AGN model with previous studies. Especially, this study combined the GHR’s state estimation results with the GRU time prediction algorithm (GHR+GRU) to achieve forecasting task and extended the GGNN from water quality to the task of nodal demand.\nFor all three models (AGN, GGNN, and GRU), the hidden layer dimension was set to 12. All three neural networks were trained using the ADAM optimizer with a training batch size of 32. The initial learning rate was set to 1e-3, and it was reduced by a factor of 0.8 after 1 training epoch. If the training error does not decrease over 10 batches, the epoch will be terminated early. For the AGN and GGNN, the number of recurrent layers was set to 3. The forecasting step length is set to be equal to the entered historical step length.\n3.3. Evaluation metric\nThis study reported the Mean Absolute Error (MAE) and Mean Absolute Percentage Error (MAPE) of forecasting results for all 783 nodes (including 1 “pumping station node”) in different forecasting time steps, to evaluate the model's spatial generalization and time series forecasting effect. The formula is as follows:\n(13)\n𝑀⁢𝐴⁢𝐸=\n1\n(𝐼+𝐼*)×𝐽*\n⁢\n𝐼+𝐼*\n∑\n𝑖=1\n𝐽*\n∑\n𝑗=1\n∣ℎ𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗−\ñ\nℎ\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗∣\n(14)\n𝑀⁢𝐴⁢𝑃⁢𝐸=\n1\n(𝐼+𝐼*)×𝐽*\n⁢\n𝐼+𝐼*\n∑\n𝑖=1\n𝐽*\n∑\n𝑗=1\n∣ℎ𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗−\ñ\nℎ\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗∣\nℎ𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗\nwhere ℎ𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗\n is the prediction and \ñ\nℎ\n𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗\n is the simulated measurement of vector 𝑣𝑖\n at time step 𝑡𝑗\n.\n4. Results and discussion\nThis section presents the results from both forecasting performance and model explainability analyses. First, this study report and analyze the forecasting task results: the proposed method achieves superior spatial generalization and temporal forecasting accuracy compared to previous approaches. Subsequently, this study evaluates the robustness of the AGN model across varying prediction windows. Finally, once demonstrated the quality of our predictions, this study explains the recurrent layers, attention mechanisms and nodal features used in the model, demonstrating how these components influence the final output of the model.\n4.1. Forecasting results\n4.1.1. Spatial generalization accuracy\nThis study aims to utilize the GNN algorithm to predict the water demand states from a pressure perspective. A thorough review of previous research revealed that, although several pioneering studies have explored the estimation or forecasting of WDS states without relying on pre-built hydraulic models, they still face limitations. Specifically, the GHR method proposed by Zhou et al. [37] is restricted to instantaneous state estimation, and its accuracy heavily depends on sensor placement; the direct application of the GGNN algorithm does not integrate well with the characteristics of water networks [18]. To address these shortcomings, this study introduces the AGN method. This innovative approach incorporates the attention mechanisms into hidden state representation and node feature extraction. By doing so, it overcomes the limitations of traditional recurrent graph convolution methods and aligns closely with the unique pressure characteristics of water networks. To evaluate the performance of the AGN, the GGNN method and a GRU-enhanced GHR approach were employed as benchmark models for comparison.\nFig. 3 visually demonstrates AGN's superior performance on the test set against its benchmark counterparts: the GGNN method and the GRU-enhanced GHR (i.e. GHR+GRU) approach. Specifically, the MAE of node predictions was categorized into three regions, clearly revealing the significant advantage of the AGN model in spatial generalization accuracy. Notably, 82.8 % of total nodes have an MAE below 0.5 m, a widely recognized threshold for practical application in hydraulic model calibration. Compared to competing models, AGN shows a significant advantage in the proportion of nodes below this threshold, outperforming GGNN and GHR+GRU methods by 79 % and 73.4 %, respectively, highlighting its superior spatial generalization performance.\nFurther analysis of Fig. 3(b) reveals a profound correlation between prediction error and nodal head variation. Nodes with errors between 0.5 m and 1.0 m are mainly concentrated in high-elevation areas, while low-elevation nodes generally face challenges with MAE greater than 2.0 m. This phenomenon indicates an imbalance in model training, particularly as low-elevation nodes are not sufficiently learned due to sparse sensor placement. We attribute this primarily to two factors: (1) sensor scarcity in low-elevation regions hinders the model's ability to capture localized hydraulic characteristics, and (2) insufficient model capacity to discern elevation-driven hydraulic variations. These findings underscore the critical impact of sensor deployment density on data-driven model performance, highlighting the need for comprehensive data acquisition to optimize model training.\nDownload: Download high-res image (1MB)\nDownload: Download full-size image\n\nFig. 3. Comparison of forecasting models in spatial direction: (a) The real nodal heads variation; (b) The MAE loss of AGN model; (c) The MAE loss of GGNN model; (d) The MAE loss of GHR+GRU method.\n\nAdmittedly, in the face of complex water network components, drastic elevation changes, and sparse sensor deployment, the AGN model still faces considerable challenges in pursuing comprehensive and accurate predictions. However, it is noteworthy that even under such extreme conditions, the AGN model demonstrates remarkable predictive ability in the high-elevation area, despite the sensors in this area still being relatively sparse — 100 % of high-elevation nodes have an MAE below 1.0 m. In the context of low-density sensor deployment, the AGN can still effectively address sparse sensor environments, reducing reliance on sensing equipment. More real-world WDSs will benefit from our sensor-robust model.\nAs shown in Fig. 3(b), the AGN model achieves satisfactory prediction accuracy and breadth in the medium-elevation area. This suggests that in water distribution systems (WDSs) characterized by relatively gentle terrain and moderate head changes, the AGN model can deliver enhanced predictive performance. These results not only further consolidate the practicality and reliability of the AGN model but also lays a solid theoretical and practical foundation for its widespread application in complex WDS management, heralding a promising future for data-driven technology in intelligent WDS management.\nDue to their relatively low deployment and maintenance costs, pressure sensors have been widely used in WDSs. This provides a rich data source for the AGN model and practically verifies the applicability and feasibility of water demand prediction from pressure data. This practice-oriented research perspective offers new ideas and methodological support for WDS management.\n4.1.2. Temporal forecasting accuracy\nTo further analyze the fluctuations in prediction accuracy over time, Fig. 4 presents the MAPE variation for 328 non-overlapping test batches in a time series format. This visualization enhances our understanding of the model’s dynamic performance and highlights the superior long-term effectiveness of the AGN method. In each test batch, the MAPE of AGN remains impressively below 5 %, demonstrating significant advantages in both prediction accuracy and stability over the GGNN and GHR+GRU methods.\nDownload: Download high-res image (373KB)\nDownload: Download full-size image\n\nFig. 4. Comparison of forecasting models in temporal direction.\n\nNotably, the AGN model maintains stable and low MAPE levels even after more than a month of continuous testing. This remarkable performance is a strong testament to its robustness in time-series prediction. This stability is also crucial for practical applications, allowing the model to operate effectively long-term and significantly reduce dependence on computational resources.\nFurther comparative analysis reveals that, unlike AGN and GGNN which also incorporate GRU components, the GHR+GRU method exhibits significant instability in its time-series performance, with markedly increased fluctuation amplitudes. This phenomenon may indicate the limitations of the GHR method in handling water networks with extreme head variations and suboptimal sensor placement. It emphasizes the spatial generalization challenges of accurately predicting non-sensor nodes’ state based on limited sensor measurements. And this is the key challenge in data-driven water network modeling.\nAlthough this study achieves high-precision predictions with MAPE not exceeding 5 % over time, it seems unacceptable when faced with a wide head variation range of 41.1 m to 113.6 m (Fig. 3(a)). This is mainly due to the approximately 4 % of low-elevation nodes, whose MAPE reaches as high as 80 %, significantly raising the overall average MAPE. The error levels for different elevation regions are analyzed in detail in the next section.\nDespite these limitations, the results of the demand prediction displayed along the time axis clearly show the long-term robustness of the AGN model in practical deployment environments. Notably, the model was trained on a consumer-grade GPU platform (NVIDIA GeForce RTX 4060 Ti), requiring approximately 3 h in total. Leveraging the computational efficiency inherent to deep learning methods, the model achieves an inference time of <1 s per run, sufficiently satisfies the requirement for real-time prediction. These findings not only enhance our confidence in the model’s performance but also provides strong support and insights for its future application in broader and more complex scenarios. Subsequent sections analyze prediction accuracy variations across time steps and regions.\n4.1.3. Accuracy analysis at varying time steps\nTo thoroughly investigate the prediction accuracy of the proposed AGN model across three different elevation regions with varying prediction steps, we tested four different prediction steps and presented the MAE and MAPE for the low, medium, and high regions separately (Table 1). As the prediction step length increased, the error metrics for all three regions exhibited stability, with minimal variation observed. The overall error was primarily contributed by the fewer low-elevation nodes. The medium- and high-elevation nodes achieved satisfactory prediction results.\n\nTable 1. Forecasting with different prediction window sizes.\n\nForecasting steps\tRegion\tMAE (m)\tMAPE ( %)\n12\tHigh\t0.859\t1.12\nMedium\t0.327\t0.439\nLow\t32.7\t79.7\n24\tHigh\t0.885\t1.21\nMedium\t0.329\t0.441\nLow\t32.7\t79.6\n48\tHigh\t0.725\t0.985\nMedium\t0.370\t0.496\nLow\t32.6\t79.3\n96\tHigh\t0.692\t0.938\nMedium\t0.396\t0.531\nLow\t32.5\t79.0\nDetailed analysis of prediction accuracy across different regions at varying time steps revealed a distinct trend: as the prediction step length increased, prediction error for high-elevation nodes decreased, while error for medium-elevation nodes increased. This indicates that for high-elevation nodes experiencing data scarcity, extending the time series length and incorporating more prior temporal information enhances forecast accuracy. Conversely, for nodes in data-rich areas, additional temporal information may introduce unnecessary noise or redundancy, reducing model accuracy. This finding suggests that, for nodes in data-scarce conditions, incorporating more prior information and adopting more suitable data fusion techniques will be key to enhancing the model’s performance.\n4.2. Results explanation\n4.2.1. Rule extraction from recurrent layer\nEmploying recurrent graph convolution (as shown in Eq. (5)), the AGN model gradually expands the receptive field of sensor nodes’ neighboring nodes. Simultaneously, pressure feature transfer from sensor nodes to global nodes is achieved by the self-attention mechanism, enabling long-range generalization.\nTo understand the rule of recurrent layers and their impact on the model’s generalization ability, this study varied the number of recurrent layers and tested the model’s generalization accuracy under different iterations. Fig. 5(a) shows the relative position relationship between sensor nodes and non-sensor nodes. The size and color depth of the non-sensor nodes indicate their distance from the nearest sensor nodes. Additionally, the data statistics in the upper right corner reveal the shortest path distribution between sensor nodes and non-sensor nodes. Notably, most non-sensor nodes are three steps away from sensor nodes, with the longest path being eleven steps. This information provides a crucial basis for subsequent analysis.\nDownload: Download high-res image (827KB)\nDownload: Download full-size image\n\nFig. 5. Analysis of the number of the recurrent layers: (a) shortest path from sensor nodes to non-sensor nodes; (b) MAE distribution corresponding to different numbers of the recurrent layers; (c) MAPE corresponding to different numbers of the recurrent layers.\n\nExperimental results (Figs. 5(b) and 5(c)) indicate that the best performance is not achieved at the maximum iteration of 11, but at 3 iterations of recurrent layers. This finding demonstrates that more iterations are not necessarily better; instead, they need to match the majority of the shortest path between sensor nodes and non-sensor nodes. When the number of iterations exceeds a certain threshold (such as 1 in this case), further increasing the number of recurrent layers has limited improvement on the model’s prediction performance. This is primarily due to the attention mechanism’s ability to capture long-distance correlations, allowing nodes to transcend physical distance limitations. Through the self-attention mechanism, the model can learn and transfer the pressure characteristics from sensor nodes to all nodes, rather than being limited to the sensor nodes’ neighborhood. Therefore, spatial generalizing process can be summarized as two significant mechanisms: neighbor topology feature transferring based on graph convolution, and global feature generalization based on the self-attention mechanism. Based on these two key mechanisms, the AGN model can generalize the learned graph structure-related features to all nodes in WDS, achieving state forecasting.\nIn the GGNN, ensuring sensor nodes can “perceive” the farthest non-sensor nodes theoretically requires up to 11 steps of recurrent iterations. However, this approach presents significant practical challenges, particularly due to the substantial increase in training time. For example, training with 11 iterations of recurrent layers on an A10 tensor core GPU in the small-scale network studied here takes about two days. This duration is unacceptable for practical applications in large-scale WDSs, where non-sensor nodes are further away from their nearest sensor node. In contrast, using 3 recurrent layers reduces the training time to about 3 h, demonstrating significant efficiency advantages.\nFurther analysis reveals that increasing the number of recurrent layers from 1 to 2 significantly reduces the MAPE. However, increasing beyond 3 layers leads to a rise in MAPE. This suggests that too many recurrent layers may introduce excessive non-sensor node features, causing data redundancy or pollution, and weakening the model’s prediction performance. Additionally, regardless of the number of recurrent layers, the cumulative maximum MAE remains stable at 96.03 %, revealing an optimization bottleneck in specific regions, such as low-elevation region in this study. These regions, due to insufficient sensor placement, cannot improve training results by increasing the number of recurrent layers.\nIn summary, incorporating the self-attention mechanism in the recurrent layers significantly enhances the model’s prediction accuracy. It also effectively reduces the iteration of recurrent layers, greatly decreasing training time of the model. This advantage is crucial for predictive modeling of actual WDSs, especially for medium- and large-scale systems, where its efficiency will provide significant application value.\n4.2.2. Hidden semantic analysis of gating mechanisms\nThrough an in-depth analysis of the weight matrices obtained from the trained reset gate (Eq. (6)) and the update gate (Eq. (7)), this study reveals the unique mechanism by which the model selectively retains short- and long-term information between nodes. Analysis of the model parameters reveals that the weights of the two critical gates consistently remain close to 0.5. The balance between the reset gate and update gate indicates that short- and long-term memory are equally important in the forecasting model. This finding inspires a new perspective on model simplification: replacing complex gating mechanisms with several constants. This operation will significantly reduce the model’s parameters, thereby enhancing its performance and facilitating its application in larger-scale WDSs.\nThe CHPS (Eq. (8)) demonstrates how the AGN model efficiently retains key information. By visually mapping the attention weight of the hidden state layer onto the network (Fig. 6), we can observe how sensor nodes influence non-sensor nodes and how the model selectively focuses on feature correlation. For visualization purposes, only edges with weights greater than 0.01 are displayed in the figure, and the weights of nodes 215 were divided by 10. This analysis deepens our understanding of the model’s internal hidden mechanisms and highlights the impact of sensor layout on prediction performance.\nDownload: Download high-res image (2MB)\nDownload: Download full-size image\n\nFig. 6. Attention weight of the CHPS.\n\nThe study found significant differences in the contributions of various sensor nodes during the prediction process, reflecting their interdependence and the strategic significance of sensor layout. Intuitively, sensor nodes in central areas (e.g., 458, 549, and 752) enjoy higher weights due to their critical positions, significantly enhancing the overall prediction performance. In contrast, sensors at the network periphery (e.g., nodes 114, 469, and 769) are less important. The concepts of geographical center and periphery do not exist in graph theory; only the concept of connectivity does. In other words, nodes that exhibit better connectivity or play a crucial role in the network’s connectivity were trained with higher weights. Placing sensor nodes at critical connectivity points appears to be key to improving the performance of graph-based data-driven models. The importance related to node connectivity underscores the need to optimize sensor layouts, especially in selecting critical connections. However, further cross-network studies are needed to definitively establish the correlation between sensor placement and connectivity.\nBy introducing the self-attention mechanism into the gating mechanism, this study broadens the receptive field of sensor nodes and effectively captures global information. Additionally, it deepens our understanding of the working mechanisms of complex network prediction models. These findings provide theoretical support for optimizing existing models and lay a solid foundation for developing new models for future urban infrastructure monitoring and management.\n4.2.3. Attribution analysis based on nodal features\nBy separately masking nodal height and nodal type features, this study analyzed their independent and synergistic effects on the model’s prediction. Fig. 7 shows the prediction effects after masking these two features in the AGN model. The fourth quadrant of the figure summarizes the MAE and MAPE statistics for each masking scenario, providing quantitative evidence of feature contributions. The AGN model in Fig. 7(a), with both nodal type and height masked, is a modification of the GGNN (Fig. 3(c)), replacing all fully connected layers with self-attention layers. Experimental results show that the attention mechanism significantly expands the model’s receptive field, enabling 50.1 % of the nodes to be trained — a 14.7 % improvement over the original GGNN. These findings highlight the attention mechanism’s advantage in capturing long-distance correlations between nodes.\nDownload: Download high-res image (1009KB)\nDownload: Download full-size image\n\nFig. 7. Nodal features analysis: (a) Mask nodal type and nodal elevation features; (b) Mask nodal type features; (c) Mask nodal elevation features.\n\nAdding any individual nodal feature to the “AGN(-type)(-height)” scenario can significantly enhance the model’s overall prediction accuracy. Specifically, adding only the nodal height feature (Fig. 7(b)) increases the proportion of nodes with MAE<1.0 m from 0 % to 95.6 %. This indicates that height, a key indicator of spatial location information, provides extensive and fundamental predictive support for the model. Adding nodal type features (Fig. 7(c)) increases the proportion of nodes with MAE<0.5 m from 0 % to 23.2 %. This significantly enhances the model’s ability to capture specific nodal characteristics and improves prediction accuracy, demonstrating the unique value of type features in refining predictions. Both nodal elevation and type features offer distinct and complementary contributions to model performance. The results highlight the critical role of prior network information in improving model performance.\nHowever, in water networks lacking detailed nodal information, obtaining accurate nodal height and type data can be challenging. Therefore, promoting and deepening the AGN model in practical applications will require diversified model assistance and data substitution strategies. For instance, nodal height can be approximated using surface elevation, and nodal type can be inferred by integrating urban AOI data with network topology. With the inclusion and integration of more network prior information, the AGN model can better align with water network characteristics, becoming powerful tools for the safety management and distribution optimization of WDSs.\n5. Conclusions\nThis study proposes a novel multi-step demand forecasting solution for cities lacking hydraulic mo"
  }
]
```

def tex(s):
 return ''.join({'\\':r'\textbackslash{}','&':r'\&','%':r'\%','$':r'\$','#':r'\#','_':r'\_','{':r'\{','}':r'\}','~':r'\textasciitilde{}','^':r'\textasciicircum{}'}.get(c,c) for c in s)
class HTMLTex(HTMLParser):
 def __init__(self):super().__init__();self.parts=[]
 def handle_starttag(self,tag,attrs):
  attr=dict(attrs)
  if tag=='b':self.parts.append(r'\textbf{')
  elif tag=='font':self.parts.append(r'\texttt{')
  elif tag=='link':self.parts.append(r'\href{'+attr['href']+'}{')
  elif tag=='br':self.parts.append(r'\par ')
 def handle_endtag(self,tag):
  if tag in ['b','font','link']:self.parts.append('}')
 def handle_data(self,s):self.parts.append(tex(s))
def html(s):p=HTMLTex();p.feed(s.replace('v0.1','v'+version));return ''.join(p.parts)

PUB=WEB/'public/miniproject/milestone-2'
OUT.mkdir(parents=True,exist_ok=True)

def digest(p):return hashlib.sha256(p.read_bytes()).hexdigest()
def dump(p,data):p.write_text(json.dumps(data,ensure_ascii=False,indent=2),encoding='utf-8')
def p(text):return {'kind':'p','args':[text]}
def h(text):return {'kind':'h','args':[text]}
def img(name,caption,height):return {'kind':'image','args':[name,caption,height]}
def table(rows,widths):return {'kind':'table','args':[rows,widths]}
def eq(text):return {'kind':'math','args':[text]}
def savefig(fig,name):
 for ext in ['png','svg']:
  fig.savefig(OUT/f'{name}.{ext}',dpi=220,bbox_inches='tight',facecolor='white')
 shutil.copyfile(OUT/f'{name}.png',PUB/f'{name}.png')
 plt.close(fig)

def corpus_audit():
 source_path=WEB/'src/miniproject/classification-data.json'
 data=json.loads(source_path.read_text(encoding='utf-8'))
 assert len(data['sources'])==59 and len(data['families'])==43
 assert Counter(s['kind'] for s in data['sources'])=={'pdf':46,'repository':11,'editorial':2}
 fam={f['id']:f for f in data['families']}
 assert len({s['id'] for s in data['sources']})==59
 ids=[s for f in data['families'] for s in f['sources']]
 assert len(ids)==59 and len(set(ids))==59
 rows=[]
 for s in data['sources']:
  target=PROJECT/s['file'] if s['file'] else None
  exists=target.is_file() if target else None
  assert target is None or exists, str(target)
  sha=digest(target) if target else None
  if s['kind']=='pdf':assert sha==s['sha256'],str(target)
  if s['kind']=='repository':
   provenance=json.loads(target.read_text(encoding='utf-8'))
   assert provenance.get('sha',provenance.get('commit'))==s['commit']
  rows.append({'source_id':s['id'],'kind':s['kind'],'family':s['family'],
   'primary_goal':fam[s['family']]['primary_goal'],'scope':fam[s['family']]['scope'],
   'forecast_status':fam[s['family']]['forecast_status'],'url':s['url'],
   'local_file':s['file'],'exists':exists,'verified_file_sha256':sha,
   'commit':s.get('commit'),'evidence_locator':s['evidence']})
 goals=Counter(f['primary_goal'] for f in data['families'])
 confirmed=[f['id'] for f in data['families'] if f['forecast_status']=='confirmed']
 lt=[f for f in data['families'] if f['scope']=='ltown']
 assert set(confirmed)=={'chebnet','sam','aignn','hda'}
 summary={'frozen_inventory_cutoff':data['cutoff'],'audit_date':'2026-10-15',
   'source_sha256':digest(source_path),'records':59,'pdfs':46,'repositories':11,'editorial_partial':2,
   'families':43,'goals':dict(goals),'scope_counts':dict(Counter(f['scope'] for f in data['families'])),
   'confirmed':confirmed,'confirmed_all_percent':100*4/43,
   'confirmed_ltown_percent_all_families':100*3/43,
   'confirmed_ltown_percent_ltown_families':100*3/len(lt),
   'counting_rule':'One primary goal per grouped record. Versions, papers and code share a family. Families include reviews/support; not independent empirical studies.',
   'screening_limit':'Original search engine hit counts, full exclusion counts and duplicate-removal stage totals were not retained. No PRISMA or recall estimate can be reconstructed.',
   'source_rows':rows}
 dump(OUT/'CORPUS_AUDIT.json',summary)
 with (OUT/'audit_sources.csv').open('w',encoding='utf-8-sig',newline='') as f:
  w=csv.DictWriter(f,fieldnames=list(rows[0]));w.writeheader();w.writerows(rows)
 shutil.copyfile(OUT/'audit_sources.csv',PUB/'revision_audit_sources.csv')
 return data,summary

 c.set_title('C. Component audit: independent of the final purpose',loc='left',fontweight='bold',fontsize=12)
 c.set_xlabel('43 grouped records; confirmed future pressure = 4/43 (9.3%)')
 c.grid(axis='x',alpha=.14);c.set_axisbelow(True)
 savefig(fig,'review_search_summary')

PROFILE 
=== PDF PAGE 6 ===
Case Study 2
Study Area and Datasets
A water distribution network provided from the Battle of Leakage
Detection and Isolation Methods 2020 (BattleDIM 2020) was
taken as the second example, and the application of the proposed
EILF is demonstrated here. Compared with case study one, case
study two has more abundant data. As shown in Fig. 5, WDS in
L-TOWN covers 42.6 km of underground pipes that serve a total
population size of 10,000, including both the residential and com-
mercial domains. Pressure and flow sensors are set up at a few
predefined locations in the network. The L-town network is
generally divided into three key areas: area A comprises the res-
idential and commercial facilities, area B comprises a pressure
reduction valve to reduce the incipient leakages, and area C com-
prises a pump and water tank to supply enough pressure to the
consumers in that area and is also equipped with 82 water demand
reading sensors. Also, 33 pressure sensors and 2 flow sensors
were deployed across the most sensitive node locations within the
network. These historical data of L-town have been collected for
the period January 1, 2018 0:00 until December 31, 2019 23:55
at 5-min time steps. Several leakages occurred in L-town since
the 2018 datasets have a clear leak label, data in 2018 were used
for model development, and data in 2019 were used for testing
the EILF.
Performance of Estimation
According to the layout of the L-town water distribution network
and the locations of the sensors, the network is divided into two
relatively independent areas. Area C has sensors for total inflow,
and most nodes in the area have water meters (AMRs) at the same
time, which is regarded as one zone. Area A and B are treated as
Fig. 5. Spatial distribution of L-town network.
Fig. 4. Comparison of leak detection between raw residual series and 5-min difference residual series: (a) the Z score of raw residual series and 5-min
difference residual series; (b) the statistical boxes of raw residual series and 5-min difference residual series; and (c) the statistical boxes of Z score
calculated by raw residual series and 5-min difference residual series.

=== PDF PAGE 7 ===
one zone, which is called area AB, because there is no separate
sensor measuring the inflow to area B. The flow residuals in
area C and area AB were calculated separately.
For area C, the difference between the total inflow and total
water consumption can reflect the leak information. The total in-
flow is calculated via the water level in the tank and the flow data of
the pump that delivers water to the Tank. It should be noted that the
level data of the tank has not changed before and after some time
point, and the pump at this time also stopped running. This seems to
suggest no water usage in the area, yet it contradicts the data from
the AMRs that show evident water consumption in that period. This
implies inaccuracy in the level data of the tower. To minimize its
adverse impact on the detection of leakage events in area C, the
total inflow and total demand in area C are calculated at intervals
of 30 min.
The total inflow to the area AB is recorded by three flow sen-
sors. The actual water consumption of users in the area AB needs
to be estimated. Considering the relatively complete monitoring of
junction consumption behavior in area C, this can be used as the
nonleakage DMA data to extract the seasonal trend of the L-town.
Specifically, the changing trend of usersâ€™ water usage mode in
area C during the year should be similar to that in area AB.
Therefore, the seasonal trend in the data in area C can be trans-
planted to the prediction of water demand in area AB. The weekly
pattern of area AB can be predicted by combining it with the sim-
ulation in one week of the hydraulic model. The main solution here
is to analyze the trend of demand mode in area C, the flow series of
2018 and 2019 are decomposed by EMD. The seasonal change
trend of water consumption obtained by EMD is shown in Fig. 6.
The figure also shows the value of the 1st/3rd/5th intrinsic mode
function (IMF) (Hu et al. 2021). Each IMF represents the compo-
nent of a certain frequency characteristic in the original signal.
The interannual variation of water consumption can be expressed
by residual. From the interannual point of view, the growth of
water consumption is slow, and the water consumption in winter
is less than that in summer. After that, the inflow could be estimated
through the EMD seasonal component and demand pattern of
DMA, and the result of estimation is shown in Fig. 7. Besides,
the time series trend decomposition model to estimate the trend
item of the pressure series. The pressure trend series is decomposed
by additive mode STL. When different leaks occur, the pressures
of multiple nodes will change in different magnitudes, as shown
in Fig. 8.
Performance of Identification
The pressure and flow changes resulting from the leak are described
by the estimated flow residual series and pressure trend series.
For the flow of area AB, it is intuitive to calculate the residuals
between inflow and estimated flow for identifying leakages. For
the flow of area C, data observed from the pump sensor and the
tank level meter are used to calculate the total inflow of area C,
and the sum of AMR demands gives the aggregate user consump-
tions. The difference between total inflow and user consumptions is
used to identify leakages as residuals in area C.
As shown in Fig. 7(b), although the residuals series is calculated
by the difference between observed and estimated series, it is still
a time series with irregular fluctuations. The irregular fluctuation
proves the importance of taking difference and smoothing methods.
It is worth noting that the difference operation is very sensitive to
the high-frequency components of the data, special attention should
be paid to the influence of noise data. Before the difference oper-
ation, fuzzy noise reduction processing is generally required. To
reduce the nonstationary of residual series, moving average is cal-
culated to make the data smooth, the smoothed flow residual in
2019 Area AB is shown in Fig. 9(a).
Fig. 6. Results of flow series decomposition by EMD method: (a) the 2018 seasonal trend of water consumption obtained by EMD; and (b) the 2019
seasonal trend of water consumption obtained by EMD.

[Fig. 9(e)]. The abrupt increase and decrease are easily identified
through difference transformation, three-sigma SPC is used to iden-
tify the sudden rise and incline of the flow curve. Therefore, the
start time (abrupt increase), end time of leakages (abrupt decrease),
and leak size (the abrupt value of increase or decrease) are obtained,
and the results of 2019 leakage identification are shown in Fig. 9,
where the red line represents the abrupt leakages, and the blue line
represents the incipient leakages. It can be seen from the figure
that as the differential interval increases, the accuracy of detection
becomes higher and higher. This is because the difference is greatly
affected by the smoothing of the data. When the difference interval
is small, the data fluctuates greatly, and the leaked data is easily
covered by random items.
There is one more thing to figure out, how does this start time
match the end time. The pressure method to address this problem
is to rank the pressure nodes residuals, as shown in Fig. 10. A
sudden increase in pressure will lead to an increase in the residual
between the current value and its moving average. The variation of
the pressure at the neighboring nodes will be higher than more
distant nodes. The birth and fix of a certain leakage will cause
similar patterns for the neighbor nodes and the distant nodes.
By observing the pattern at each start time and end time, matching
could be done.
For incipient leakages, a sliding-window-based SPC slope
method is employed to identify the incipient increase in 1-week
difference residuals, as shown in Fig. 9(e). If without leakages,
the curve of residuals is likely to be smooth and constant. The pat-
tern of flow residuals consists of many subpatterns caused by differ-
ent leakages. After the identification of abrupt leakages, the pattern
caused by abrupt leakages could be removed from the curve of
residuals. The rest of the curve only contains the subpattern caused
by the incipient leakages. Their slopes could be used to distin-
guish different unfixed incipient leakages. Each incipient leakage
has an identical and constant slope. In this way, the start time of
each incipient leakage could be identified once the slope of the
curve changes. A sliding window is used to go through the curve.
As shown in Table 1, all the unfixed incipient leakage could be
identified with the aforementioned method. However, the peak of
each unfixed incipient leakage is hard to be determined because
of the overlap of different incipient leakage. Therefore, an approxi-
mate peak time is given for each incipient leakage.
Fig. 7. Flow estimation in Area AB: (a) estimation of the flow; and (b) residual between estimated and observed flow.
Fig. 8. Pressure trend components in Area AB.

Performance of Localization
Due to the fact that the boundaries of the three regions of the
L-Town network are obvious, it is easy to build hydraulic models
of area C as it has a large number of AMRs, so we split the
L-Town network into two parts, area C network and area AB
network, to locate leakages separately. We first delete the pump
connecting area AB and area C so that the original L-Town net-
work is divided. For the area AB network, we add a node at the
position of the pump with a demand pattern based on pump flow
SCADA data, and we replace the tank with a reservoir that is

=== PDF PAGE 10 ===
assigned a head pattern using the observed tank level data in
area C. Finally, we build two separated networks (areas AB
and C) to locate leakages with the same hydraulic calculation re-
sults as the L-Town network.
The hydraulic model of L-town built on the EPANET tool is
employed to simulate nodal pressure. The pressure sensorsâ€™ data
without leakages in 2018 is used to calibrate two networks by ad-
justing the roughness of pipes to minimize the mean squared error
(MSE) of observed and simulated pressure values.
For area AB, it is difficult to build an accurate hydraulic model
as the network has no AMRs, so we use the idea of control variates
to localize each leakage in this area. First, we find two weeks when
the leakage had occurred and the leakage had not occurred based on
the start and end time of the leakage which is calculated before.
Second, making a difference between the observed pressure data
at the corresponding time in the two weeks. We assume that the
localized leakage is the only leakage between the two weeks, so
the difference could reflect the effect of this leakage on pressure
sensors in the network. Third, two new area AB networks are cre-
ated to indicate the two weeks mentioned by adjusting demands
with season trends and adding pump flow based on SCADA data,
and some known unfixed leakages are added to the network as well.
Then grid searching is conducted for nodes and emitter coefficient.
Nodes are searched one by one in the area AB network except for
reservoir nodes. Emitter coefficients that are used to simulate leak-
ages are searched at appropriate intervals (based on the size of the
leakage flow, i.e. bigger leakage flow has bigger search intervals).
A difference between the simulated pressure data is calculated in
the same way as before for the observed data. Finally, the MSE of
two differences, the observed valuesâ€™ difference, and the simulated
valuesâ€™ difference are used as an objective function for this prob-
lem, and we could find a high probability area of leakage with a low
MSE value.
In area C, almost every node has an AMR, facilitating the
accurate building of a hydraulic model in EPANET. We select
one week when the bust had been occurred and compare the
observed and the simulated values to determine the location of
the leakage. Second, we assign the AMR demand data to nodes
and calculate the reservoir head pattern according to the SCADA
data of tank level. Third, search for nodes and emitter coeffi-
cients and find the difference between the observed pressure
value and the simulated value. Finally, we use the same way
to calculate the MSE of the difference and find a high probable
area of leakage.
Fig. 11 shows the result of the leakage that occurred on April 20,
2019, 12:00. The shade in grey in the figure represents the size
of the MSE. The node with darker color has a smaller MSE.
Among them, red, orange, yellow, green, and blue indicate the five
nodes with the smallest MSE, that is, the area with the highest prob-
ability of leakage.
In addition, the performance of the proposed weekly difference
localization method and traditional method are compared in this
section. The distinction between the two methods is whether to
carry out the weekly difference operation. Two metrics are calcu-
lated to evaluate the effectiveness of localization: (1) the distance
between the real leak hotspot and the hotspot found by the
model (m); and (2) the interval number of pipes between the real
leak hotspot and the hotspot found by the model. As shown in
Fig. 12, the trend of the results of the two metrics is basically the
same. Among the 9 leak hotspots in 2018, the proposed localization
method has better positioning accuracy on 7 leak hotspots than tra-
ditional methods. Both methods have the same performance and
accuracy in finding the leak hotspot (3-5). The results of the tradi-
tional method are only slightly better than the proposed methods at
the leak hotspot 1-31. From the perspective of positioning distance,
all of the errors localized by the proposed method are less than
300m, the average localized error is 100.7 m, while the average
error of the traditional method is 385.3m, and the errors of 5 leak
hotspots are greater than 400 m. In addition, the performance of the
traditional method and the proposed weekly difference localization
method for the leakage on October 26, 2018, are shown in Fig. 13.
Red represents the pipe with the highest probability of leakage. The
proposed method could accurately locate the area of the leakage,
while the traditional method has a large error. In general, the pro-
posed weekly difference method has better performance than the
traditional method. In the traditional method, because the model
simulation value and the observation value are directly used for
comparison, the error of the hydraulic model cannot be eliminated.
In the proposed method, the weekly difference of the data can
eliminate the error of the hydraulic model, so better results have
been achieved.

and the hotspot found by the model (m); and (b) the interval number

Conclusions
In this work, a novel framework called EILF is proposed for timely,
accurate, and effective detection and isolation of overlapping leak-
ages. Flow and pressure features are highlighted through the esti-
mation stage, multiple abrupt and incipient leakages are detected
through the identification stage, and the leak hotspots are local-
ized through an improved differential search localization stage.
The multiscale differential operation, a simple but efficient math-
ematical method, is employed to effectively extract leak-induced
mutation, reduce uncertainty, and improve localizing accuracy in
the WDSs. Two case studies, a real-life network and an L-town
network, are applied to demonstrate the proposed method. The con-
clusions are drawn as follows:
1. Applying the empirical mode decomposition and time series de-
composition, accurate estimations of flow under the normal con-
dition and pressure-drop trend are used to extract leak-induced
values from the monitoring data. Rather than analyzing original
flow and pressure signals directly, this study highlights the es-
sential leak characteristics that reduce the impact of diurnal var-
iations and provides a better solution for leak detection.
2. Multiscale difference series are calculated to reduce the nonsta-
tionary random item of residuals, the residual difference series
are used for leak identification. Similarly, the weekly difference
idea is employed to design the objective function for the locali-
zation searching problem. This approach improves the ability to
deal with uncertainty.
3. The equality of the variation of the flow residuals and the rank-
ing of pressure nodes are used to match the start time and the
end time of an abrupt leakage. Meanwhile, the sliding window-
based variation method is proposed to detect the start time and
leak size of incipient leakages. The EILF method provided an
effective multiple overlapping leak detection framework.
4. The method detects and localizes 16 leakages in 2019 with a
true positive (TPR) of 47.83%, a false positive of 5, and an eco-
nomic score of â‚¬167,981. A comparative result shows that EILF
achieved a good grade in BattleDIM 2020, demonstrating the
effectiveness of the solution for leak detection and localization
in water distribution systems. The application of this method in
real-life networks also provides a good example for rapid leak
detection in data-driven networks. The result shows that the
EILF shows good prospects for application in water distribution
systems.
Data Availability Statement
The case data and the model used in this study can be made avail-
able from BattleDIM 2020, and the codes for the proposed method
in Spyder3.7 can be made available by the corresponding author by
request.
Acknowledgments
This work was supported by the National Natural Science Founda-
tion of China (Grant No. 51879139).

Water Resour. Plann. Manage.\\r\\n J. Water Resour. Plann. Manage., 2022, 148(6): 04022021 \\r\\n Downloaded from ascelibrary.org by New Jersey Institute of Technology on 10/15/26. \\r\\nCopyright ASCE; all rights reserved, including rights for text and data mining and training of artificial intelligence or similar technologies. \\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n=== PDF PAGE 7 ===\\r\\none zone, which is called area AB, because there is no separate\\r\\nsensor measuring the inflow to area B. The flow residuals in\\r\\narea C and area AB were calculated separately.\\r\\nFor area C, the difference between the total inflow and total\\r\\nwater consumption can reflect the leak information. The total in-\\r\\nflow is calculated via the water level in the tank and the flow data of\\r\\nthe pump that delivers water to the Tank. It should be noted that the\\r\\nlevel data of the tank has not changed before and after some time\\r\\npoint, and the pump at this time also stopped running. This seems to\\r\\nsuggest no water usage in the area, yet it contradicts the data from\\r\\nthe AMRs that show evident water consumption in that period. This\\r\\nimplies inaccuracy in the level data of the tower. To minimize its\\r\\nadverse impact on the detection of leakage events in area C, the\\r\\ntotal inflow and total demand in area C are calculated at intervals\\r\\nof 30 min.\\r\\nThe total inflow to the area AB is recorded by three flow sen-\\r\\nsors. The actual water consumption of users in the area AB needs\\r\\nto be estimated. Considering the relatively complete monitoring of\\r\\njunction consumption behavior in area C, this can be used as the\\r\\nnonleakage DMA data to extract the seasonal trend of the L-town.\\r\\nSpecifically, the changing trend of usersâ€™ water usage mode in\\r\\narea C during the year should be similar to that in area AB.\\r\\nTherefore, the seasonal trend in the data in area C can be trans-\\r\\nplanted to the prediction of water demand in area AB. The weekly\\r\\npattern of area AB can be predicted by combining it with the sim-\\r\\nulation in one week of the hydraulic model. The main solution here\\r\\nis to analyze the trend of demand mode in area C, the flow series of\\r\\n2018 and 2019 are decomposed by EMD. The seasonal change\\r\\ntrend of water consumption obtained by EMD is shown in Fig. 6.\\r\\nThe figure also shows the value of the 1st/3rd/5th intrinsic mode\\r\\nfunction (IMF) (Hu et al. 2021). Each IMF represents the compo-\\r\\nnent of a certain frequency characteristic in the original signal.\\r\\nThe interannual variation of water consumption can be expressed\\r\\nby residual. From the interannual point of view, the growth of\\r\\nwater consumption is slow, and the water consumption in winter\\r\\nis less than that in summer. After that, the inflow could be estimated\\r\\nthrough the EMD seasonal component and demand pattern of\\r\\nDMA, and the result of estimation is shown in Fig. 7. Besides,\\r\\nthe time series trend decomposition model to estimate the trend\\r\\nitem of the pressure series. The pressure trend series is decomposed\\r\\nby additive mode STL. When different leaks occur, the pressures\\r\\nof multiple nodes will change in different magnitudes, as shown\\r\\nin Fig. 8.\\r\\nPerformance of Identification\\r\\nThe pressure and flow changes resulting from the leak are described\\r\\nby the estimated flow residual series and pressure trend series.\\r\\nFor the flow of area AB, it is intuitive to calculate the residuals\\r\\nbetween inflow and estimated flow for identifying leakages. For\\r\\nthe flow of area C, data observed from the pump sensor and the\\r\\ntank level meter are used to calculate the total inflow of area C,\\r\\nand the sum of AMR demands gives the aggregate user consump-\\r\\ntions. The difference between total inflow and user consumptions is\\r\\nused to identify leakages as residuals in area C.\\r\\nAs shown in Fig. 7(b), although the residuals series is calculated\\r\\nby the difference between observed and estimated series, it is still\\r\\na time series with irregular fluctuations. The irregular fluctuation\\r\\nproves the importance of taking difference and smoothing methods.\\r\\nIt is worth noting that the difference operation is very sensitive to\\r\\nthe high-frequency components of the data, special attention should\\r\\nbe paid to the influence of noise data. Before the difference oper-\\r\\nation, fuzzy noise reduction processing is generally required. To\\r\\nreduce the nonstationary of residual series, moving average is cal-\\r\\nculated to make the data smooth, the smoothed flow residual in\\r\\n2019 Area AB is shown in Fig. 9(a).\\r\\nFig. 6. Results of flow series decomposition by EMD method: (a) the 2018 seasonal trend of water consumption obtained by EMD; and (b) the 2019\\r\\nseasonal trend of water consumption obtained by EMD.\\r\\nÂ© ASCE\\r\\n04022021-7\\r\\nJ. Water Resour. Plann. Manage.\\r\\n J. Water Resour. Plann. Manage., 2022, 148(6): 04022021 \\r\\n Downloaded from ascelibrary.org by New Jersey Institute of Technology on 10/15/26. \\r\\nCopyright ASCE; all rights reserved, including rights for text and data mining and training of artificial intelligence or similar technologies. \\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n=== PDF PAGE 8 ===\\r\\nMultiscale difference is performed on the smoothed data\\r\\nset, which includes 5-min difference [Fig. 9(b)], 1-h difference\\r\\n[Fig. 9(c)], 1-day difference [Fig. 9(d)], and 1-week difference\\r\\n[Fig. 9(e)]. The abrupt increase and decrease are easily identified\\r\\nthrough difference transformation, three-sigma SPC is used to iden-\\r\\ntify the sudden rise and incline of the flow curve. Therefore, the\\r\\nstart time (abrupt increase), end time of leakages (abrupt decrease),\\r\\nand leak size (the abrupt value of increase or decrease) are obtained,\\r\\nand the results of 2019 leakage identification are shown in Fig. 9,\\r\\nwhere the red line represents the abrupt leakages, and the blue line\\r\\nrepresents the incipient leakages. It can be seen from the figure\\r\\nthat as the differential interval increases, the accuracy of detection\\r\\nbecomes higher and higher. This is because the difference is greatly\\r\\naffected by the smoothing of the data. When the difference interval\\r\\nis small, the data fluctuates greatly, and the leaked data is easily\\r\\ncovered by random items.\\r\\nThere is one more thing to figure out, how does this start time\\r\\nmatch the end time. The pressure method to address this problem\\r\\nis to rank the pressure nodes residuals, as shown in Fig. 10. A\\r\\nsudden increase in pressure will lead to an increase in the residual\\r\\nbetween the current value and its moving average. The variation of\\r\\nthe pressure at the neighboring nodes will be higher than more\\r\\ndistant nodes. The birth and fix of a certain leakage will cause\\r\\nsimilar patterns for the neighbor nodes and the distant nodes.\\r\\nBy observing the pattern at each start time and end time, matching\\r\\ncould be done.\\r\\nFor incipient leakages, a sliding-window-based SPC slope\\r\\nmethod is employed to identify the incipient increase in 1-week\\r\\ndifference residuals, as shown in Fig. 9(e). If without leakages,\\r\\nthe curve of residuals is likely to be smooth and constant. The pat-\\r\\ntern of flow residuals consists of many subpatterns caused by differ-\\r\\nent leakages. After the identification of abrupt leakages, the pattern\\r\\ncaused by abrupt leakages could be removed from the curve of\\r\\nresiduals. The rest of the curve only contains the subpattern caused\\r\\nby the incipient leakages. Their slopes could be used to distin-\\r\\nguish different unfixed incipient leakages. Each incipient leakage\\r\\nhas an identical and constant slope. In this way, the start time of\\r\\neach incipient leakage could be identified once the slope of the\\r\\ncurve changes. A sliding window is used to go through the curve.\\r\\nAs shown in Table 1, all the unfixed incipient leakage could be\\r\\nidentified with the aforementioned method. However, the peak of\\r\\neach unfixed incipient leakage is hard to be determined because\\r\\nof the overlap of different incipient leakage. Therefore, an approxi-\\r\\nmate peak time is given for each incipient leakage.\\r\\nFig. 7. Manage., 2022, 148(6): 04022021 \\r\\n Downloaded from ascelibrary.org by New Jersey Institute of Technology on 10/15/26. \\r\\nCopyright ASCE; all rights reserved, including rights for text and data mining and training of artificial intelligence or similar technologies. \\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n=== PDF PAGE 10 ===\\r\\nassigned a head pattern using the observed tank level data in\\r\\narea C. Finally, we build two separated networks (areas AB\\r\\nand C) to locate leakages with the same hydraulic calculation re-\\r\\nsults as the L-Town network.\\r\\nThe hydraulic model of L-town built on the EPANET tool is\\r\\nemployed to simulate nodal pressure. The pressure sensorsâ€™ data\\r\\nwithout leakages in 2018 is used to calibrate two networks by ad-\\r\\njusting the roughness of pipes to minimize the mean squared error\\r\\n(MSE) of observed and simulated pressure values.\\r\\nFor area AB, it is difficult to build an accurate hydraulic model\\r\\nas the network has no AMRs, so we use the idea of control variates\\r\\nto localize each leakage in this area. First, we find two weeks when\\r\\nthe leakage had occurred and the leakage had not occurred based on\\r\\nthe start and end time of the leakage which is calculated before.\\r\\nSecond, making a difference between the observed pressure data\\r\\nat the corresponding time in the two weeks. We assume that the\\r\\nlocalized leakage is the only leakage between the two weeks, so\\r\\nthe difference could reflect the effect of this leakage on pressure\\r\\nsensors in the network. Third, two new area AB networks are cre-\\r\\nated to indicate the two weeks mentioned by adjusting demands\\r\\nwith season trends and adding pump flow based on SCADA data,\\r\\nand some known unfixed leakages are added to the network as well.\\r\\nThen grid searching is conducted for nodes and emitter coefficient.\\r\\nNodes are searched one by one in the area AB network except for\\r\\nreservoir nodes. Emitter coefficients that are used to simulate leak-\\r\\nages are searched at appropriate intervals (based on the size of the\\r\\nleakage flow, i.e. bigger leakage flow has bigger search intervals).\\r\\nA difference between the simulated pressure data is calculated in\\r\\nthe same way as before for the observed data. Finally, the MSE of\\r\\ntwo differences, the observed valuesâ€™ difference, and the simulated\\r\\nvaluesâ€™ difference are used as an objective function for this prob-\\r\\nlem, and we could find a high probability area of leakage with a low\\r\\nMSE value.\\r\\nIn area C, almost every node has an AMR, facilitating the\\r\\naccurate building of a hydraulic model in EPANET. We select\\r\\none week when the bust had been occurred and compare the\\r\\nobserved and the simulated values to determine the location of\\r\\nthe leakage. Second, we assign the AMR demand data to nodes\\r\\nand calculate the reservoir head pattern according to the SCADA\\r\\ndata of tank level. Third, search for nodes and emitter coeffi-\\r\\ncients and find the difference between the observed pressure\\r\\nvalue and the simulated value. Finally, we use the same way\\r\\nto calculate the MSE of the difference and find a high probable\\r\\narea of leakage.\\r\\nFig. 11 shows the result of the leakage that occurred on April 20,\\r\\n2019, 12:00. The shade in grey in the figure represents the size\\r\\nof the MSE. The node with darker color has a smaller MSE.\\r\\nAmong them, red, orange, yellow, green, and blue indicate the five\\r\\nnodes with the smallest MSE, that is, the area with the highest prob-\\r\\nability of leakage.\\r\\nIn addition, the performance of the proposed weekly difference\\r\\nlocalization method and traditional method are compared in this\\r\\nsection. The distinction between the two methods is whether to\\r\\ncarry out the weekly difference operation. Two metrics are calcu-\\r\\nlated to evaluate the effectiveness of localization: (1) the distance\\r\\nbetween the real leak hotspot and the hotspot found by the\\r\\nmodel (m); and (2) the interval number of pipes between the real\\r\\nleak hotspot and the hotspot found by the model. As shown in\\r\\nFig. 12, the trend of the results of the two metrics is basically the\\r\\nsame. Among the 9 leak hotspots in 2018, the proposed localization\\r\\nmethod has better positioning accuracy on 7 leak hotspots than tra-\\r\\nditional methods. Both methods have the same performance and\\r\\naccuracy in finding the leak hotspot (3-5). The results of the tradi-\\r\\ntional method are only slightly better than the proposed methods at\\r\\nthe leak hotspot 1-31. From the perspective of positioning distance,\\r\\nall of the errors localized by the proposed method are less than\\r\\n300m, the average localized error is 100.7 m, while the average\\r\\nerror of the traditional method is 385.3m, and the errors of 5 leak\\r\\nhotspots are greater than 400 m. In addition, the performance of the\\r\\ntraditional method and the proposed weekly difference localization\\r\\nmethod for the leakage on October 26, 2018, are shown in Fig. 13.\\r\\nRed represents the pipe with the highest probability of leakage. The\\r\\nproposed method could accurately locate the area of the leakage,\\r\\nwhile the traditional method has a large error. In general, the pro-\\r\\nposed weekly difference method has better performance than the\\r\\ntraditional method. In the traditional method, because the model\\r\\nsimulation value and the observation value are directly used for\\r\\ncomparison, the error of the hydraulic model cannot be eliminated.\\r\\nIn the proposed method, the weekly difference of the data can\\r\\neliminate the error of the hydraulic model, so better results have\\r\\nbeen achieved.\\r\\nFig. 11. Schematic diagram of leakage isolation.\\r\\nFig. 12. Comparison of leak localization between non-difference\\r\\nmethod and week-difference method. The leaks are named by their\\r\\nstart time: month-day. For example, 1-31 represents the leak that\\r\\nstarted on January 31: (a) the distance between the real leak hotspot\\r\\nand the hotspot found by the model (m); and (b) the interval number\\r\\nof pipes between the real leak hotspot and the hotspot found by the\\r\\nmodel.\\r\\nÂ© ASCE\\r\\n04022021-10\\r\\nJ. Water Resour. Plann. Manage.\\r\\n J. Water Resour. Plann. Manage., 2022, 148(6): 04022021 \\r\\n Downloaded from ascelibrary.org by New Jersey Institute of Technology on 10/15/26. \\r\\nCopyright ASCE; all rights reserved, including rights for text and data mining and training of artificial intelligence or similar technologies. \\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n=== PDF PAGE 11 ===\\r\\nConclusions\\r\\nIn this work, a novel framework called EILF is proposed for timely,\\r\\naccurate, and effective detection and isolation of overlapping leak-\\r\\nages. Flow and pressure features are highlighted through the esti-\\r\\nmation stage, multiple abrupt and incipient leakages are detected\\r\\nthrough the identification stage, and the leak hotspots are local-\\r\\nized through an improved differential search localization stage.\\r\\nThe multiscale differential operation, a simple but efficient math-\\r\\nematical method, is employed to effectively extract leak-induced\\r\\nmutation, reduce uncertainty, and improve localizing accuracy in\\r\\nthe WDSs. Two case studies, a real-life network and an L-town\\r\\nnetwork, are applied to demonstrate the proposed method. The con-\\r\\nclusions are drawn as follows:\\r\\n1. Applying the empirical mode decomposition and time series de-\\r\\ncomposition, accurate estimations of flow under the normal con-\\r\\ndition and pressure-drop trend are used to extract leak-induced\\r\\nvalues from the monitoring data. Rather than analyzing original\\r\\nflow and pressure signals directly, this study highlights the es-\\r\\nsential leak characteristics that reduce the impact of diurnal var-\\r\\niations and provides a better solution for leak detection.\\r\\n2. Multiscale difference series are calculated to reduce the nonsta-\\r\\ntionary random item of residuals, the residual difference series\\r\\nare used for leak identification. Similarly, the weekly difference\\r\\nidea is employed to design the objective function for the locali-\\r\\nzation searching problem. This approach improves the ability to\\r\\ndeal with uncertainty.\\r\\n3. The equality of the variation of the flow residuals and the rank-\\r\\ning of pressure nodes are used to match the start time and the\\r\\nend time of an abrupt leakage. Meanwhile, the sliding window-\\r\\nbased variation method is proposed to detect the start time and\\r\\nleak size of incipient leakages. The EILF method provided an\\r\\neffective multiple overlapping leak detection framework.\\r\\n4. The method detects and localizes 16 leakages in 2019 with a\\r\\ntrue positive (TPR) of 47.83%, a false positive of 5, and an eco-\\r\\nnomic score of â‚¬167,981. A comparative result shows that EILF\\r\\nachieved a good grade in BattleDIM 2020, demonstrating the\\r\\neffectiveness of the solution for leak detection and localization\\r\\nin water distribution systems. The application of this method in\\r\\nreal-life networks also provides a good example for rapid leak\\r\\ndetection in data-driven networks. The result shows that the\\r\\nEILF shows good prospects for application in water distribution\\r\\nsystems.\\r\\nData Availability Statement\\r\\nThe case data and the model used in this study can be made avail-\\r\\nable from BattleDIM 2020, and the codes for the proposed method\\r\\nin Spyder3.7 can be made available by the corresponding author by\\r\\nrequest.\\r\\nAcknowledgments\\r\\nThis work was supported by the National Natural Science Founda-\\r\\ntion of China (Grant No. 51879139).\\r\\nReferences\\r\\nAhn, J., and D. # Milestone 2 Â· revisiÃ³n V0.7, V0.8 y V0.9

**Vigente: borrador V0.9, cinco pÃ¡ginas en inglÃ©s, no enviado.** Se conserva la plantilla del Milestone 1 y las versiones anteriores.

## QuÃ© cambiÃ³

La revisiÃ³n explica la bÃºsqueda y sus lÃ­mites, muestra cantidades y porcentajes sin duplicar versiones como estudios, justifica cada antecedente y formula una extensiÃ³n predictiva comprobable. ChebNet, SAM-kNN y AIGNN son los tres casos temporales confirmados de L-Town o modificaciones; hDS-RNN es externo. Tsinghua se destaca por el solapamiento con tanque, caudal y AMR. AGN describe demanda a partir de presiÃ³n; no se verificÃ³ una salida de presiÃ³n futura.

![Resumen de fuentes, objetivos y presiÃ³n futura](/miniproject/milestone-2/review_search_summary.png)

Los 59 registros son 46 PDF, 11 snapshots seleccionados y dos consultas editoriales parciales. Forman 43 fichas de trabajos/linajes, incluidos soporte y revisiones. Cuatro fichas confirman presiÃ³n futura (9,3% de 43); tres corresponden a L-Town/modificaciones (7,0% de 43). Una es de otra red. El porcentaje describe el corpus congelado, no toda la literatura mundial.

![TÃ©cnicas y horizontes fÃ­sicos verificados](/miniproject/milestone-2/review_techniques_horizons.png)

La lÃ­nea +15 representa nuestra propuesta. Los tres casos L-Town verifican +5 minutos; el externo +30/60/90/120. No se confunden ventana histÃ³rica, iteraciones de grafo o demora de alarma con horizonte.

## Gap y extensiÃ³n propuesta

Comparar presiÃ³n observada de n1 a +15 usando el mismo acceso a informaciÃ³n, para medir el aporte de los bloques del Ãrea C y de un proxy histÃ³rico de almacenamiento acompaÃ±ado de edades/mÃ¡scaras. Contrastar datos crudos frente al mismo modelo con estos elementos, en llenado/vaciado y retrasos auxiliares simulados. La presiÃ³n incremental se ancla en la Ãºltima n1 recibida; persistencia recibe esa misma informaciÃ³n.

No se declara inventar el pronÃ³stico de presiÃ³n, n1, el uso de T1/PUMP_1/AMR o el balance del tanque. Tsinghua ya describe ese balance para diagnÃ³stico. El aporte serÃ­a la extensiÃ³n predictiva y su evaluaciÃ³n reproducible si las comparaciones la respaldan; no hay resultados propios de modelos todavÃ­a.

## RevisiÃ³n por roles y crÃ­tica

V0.7 incorporÃ³ la sÃ­ntesis y las grÃ¡ficas; despuÃ©s se releyÃ³. V0.8 asumiÃ³ seis roles: procedencia bibliogrÃ¡fica, cÃ³digo/causalidad, hidrÃ¡ulica, estadÃ­stica, crÃ­tica de novedad y ediciÃ³n visual. V0.9 corrigiÃ³ nueve observaciones de la relectura crÃ­tica. Fue una revisiÃ³n secuencial del mismo agente, no evaluaciones independientes ni garantÃ­a absoluta de ausencia de errores.

Las tres versiones tienen cinco pÃ¡ginas, 15 enlaces y cero desbordamientos. V0.6 se preservÃ³; no se entrenaron modelos. web/src/miniproject/milestone-2\dataset.md:40:Para el proyecto de regresión las ocho tablas SCADA sí coinciden. Para separar resultados durante fugas, generar etiquetas o comparar detección, se debe usar `datos/oficial/zenodo_4017659/2019_Leakages.csv` y la configuración oficial. Una tubería con fuga puede venir de 2018: número de columnas y número de fugas que comienzan durante 2019 no son necesariamente iguales.

web/src/miniproject/milestone-2\estado-del-arte.md:54:El artículo oficial de [Vrachimis et al. (2022)](https://doi.org/10.1061/(ASCE)WR.1943-5452.0001601), p.18, fija la comparación original: Tongji TPR 56,52%, tres FP y €264.873; Under Pressure 65,22%, cuatro FP y €260.562. Las posiciones dependen de objetivos económicos y de detección; no hay un ganador universal de regresión. El artículo señala explícitamente que el benchmark omite problemas operativos como ruido de medición importante, datos descartados, retrasos y pérdidas de comunicación (p.11). **La oportunidad de estudiar disponibilidad y calidad de entradas tiene aquí respaldo directo**, aunque añadir ruido o sensores faltantes no sea una idea nueva en el campo.
web/src/miniproject/milestone-2\estado-del-arte.md:58:**LILA — Daniel et al.** Aprende relaciones contemporáneas entre presiones y términos de caudal, analiza errores con CUSUM y selecciona señales para localizar con un modelo calibrado y programación lineal/entera. Hace selección manual de períodos de referencia y combinaciones de sensores para separar fugas solapadas. Es accesible desde la regresión del curso, pero su target no es presión a t+15. La versión ampliada considera 19 fugas iniciadas en 2019, excluyendo las cuatro heredadas; Tabla 1 reporta 17 TP, dos FP, FN=0 bajo su criterio y score €307.852. Su resultado espacial hasta 374 m no significa que todas pasen el radio oficial de 300 m. La afirmación de detección rápida de fugas abruptas tiene una excepción: p280 tarda 116 h 10 min. Para una comparación justa deben conservarse el universo de eventos y las tolerancias temporales/espaciales. Véanse [artículo](https://doi.org/10.1061/(ASCE)WR.1943-5452.0001535), PDF p.4,7–9, y [código LILA](https://github.com/SWN-group-at-TU-Berlin/LILA).
web/src/miniproject/milestone-2\estado-del-arte.md:62:**SAM-kNN — Jakob et al.** Extiende kNN con memorias de corto/largo plazo y selección/adaptación de ejemplos, evaluado en diez escenarios propios de tres meses del área A modificada: cinco fugas y cinco fallas de sensor. La lectura del artículo y de `EnsembleSystem.fit` aislada deja ambigüedad temporal; `global_preprocessing` la resuelve. Con `time_win_len=4`, X promedia tres filas previas y Y toma la siguiente. Por tanto, también hay **pronóstico a un paso respecto a la última lectura, con adaptación por lotes de 200**. No debe describirse como reconstrucción solamente espacial. Detecta los cinco eventos de fuga de sus escenarios, pero sus FP son alarmas individuales y sus TP/FN se cuentan por evento; no son MAE de presión ni una evaluación original 2019. La adaptación temporal ya tiene antecedente. Véanse [artículo](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-031-15934-3_62), p.8–10, y [preprocesamiento](https://github.com/andreArtelt/SAM-kNN-Regressor_OnlineLearning_WDNs/blob/9116ec99bf9f053e35401fe3e388062e8b979c54/Implementation/LeakDbScenarioPreprocessing.py).
web/src/miniproject/milestone-2\estado-del-arte.md:64:**GSI + dictionary learning — Irofti et al.** Usa interpolación de cabeza hidráulica para crear sensores virtuales y alimentar clasificación por diccionarios; introduce múltiples diccionarios y votación para evitar que errores de interpolación deterioren el resultado. La versión conferencia genera escenarios de 30 nodos en el área A, con entrenamiento cuatro días/test tres días y magnitudes de fuga distintas. Reporta accuracy hasta 96,54% con múltiples diccionarios. No evalúa el SCADA 2019 ni presión futura. La selección de sensores virtuales puede ayudar o perjudicar: incorporar más información estimada no asegura mejora. Véase [versión publicada](https://doi.org/10.1109/CCTA49430.2022.9966160), p.4–6; el preprint se conserva como versión del mismo trabajo.
web/src/miniproject/milestone-2\estado-del-arte.md:76:**GATRes — Truong et al.** Reconstruye presiones del snapshot con atención y conexiones residuales, usando simulaciones y enmascaramiento aleatorio de sensores. Evalúa cambios de distribución, posiciones y ruido. Para L-Town, Tabla 10 (p.20) usa una semana simulada con patrones temporales: MAE 0,9501±0,0086 m, frente a 1,5928±0,0050 de mGCN y 3,4505±0,0129 de GraphConvWat. En el test de snapshots generado como train (Tabla 11) mGCN obtiene menor MAE que GATRes. Esto ilustra la sensibilidad al diseño del test. No son forecasts en 2019: la red reconstruye el estado en t. **Ni sensores faltantes ni robustez ni topología son novedosos por sí mismos.** Véanse [artículo](https://doi.org/10.1029/2023WR036741) y [código](https://github.com/DiTEC-project/gnn-pressure-estimation).
